Deligne categories and representations of the finite general linear group, part 1: universal property Thanks: 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification : 05E05, 18D10, 20C30.
Inna Entova-Aizenbud, Thorsten Heidersdorf

Abstract
. We study the Deligne interpolation categories Rep ¯ ​ ( G ​ L t ​ ( 𝔽 q ) ) {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}} for t ∈ ℂ t\in\mathbb{C} , first introduced by F. Knop . These categories interpolate the categories of finite dimensional complex representations of the finite general linear group G ​ L n ​ ( 𝔽 q ) GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) . We describe the morphism spaces in this category via generators and relations. We show that the generating object of this category ( an analogue of the representation ℂ ​ 𝔽 q n {\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n} of G ​ L n ​ ( 𝔽 q ) GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) ) carries the structure of a Frobenius algebra with a compatible 𝔽 q {\mathbb{F}}_{q} -linear structure; we call such objects 𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} -linear Frobenius spaces, and show that Rep ¯ ​ ( G ​ L t ​ ( 𝔽 q ) ) {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}} is the universal symmetric monoidal category generated by such an 𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} -linear Frobenius space of categorical dimension t t . In the second part of the paper, we prove a similar universal property for a category of representations of G ​ L ∞ ​ ( 𝔽 q ) GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) .



1.
Introduction

1.1.

An important theme in representation theory is to study representations in large rank. One incarnation of this is due to Deligne who constructed families of universal rigid symmetric monoidal categories
\underline{{\rm Rep}}(GL_{t})
,
\underline{{\rm Rep}}(O_{t})
,
\underline{{\rm Rep}}(S_{t})
,
t\in\mathbb{C}
(see
[
DM
,
D2
]
) which capture stabilization phenomena that occur when the rank of the group goes to infinity. These categories interpolate the representation categories
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
\mathrm{Rep}(O_{n}(\mathbb{C}))
,
\mathrm{Rep}(S_{n})
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
) in the sense that these
Deligne categories
admit for integral parameters
t\in\mathbb{N}
the usual representation categories as quotients. Knop (see
[
K
]
) extended Deligne’s construction and defined a vast number of interpolating categories which capture the phenomenon of stabilization with respect to rank. Among Knop’s examples are the categories of finite dimensional complex representations of the finite general linear group
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

The current article is the first paper in a series in which we study
Knop’s interpolation categories for
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, which we denote by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
.

In order to define this category, we first define a “skeletal” subcategory
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})}}
generated by a self-dual object
[1]
, which is the analogue of the
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
-representation
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
(this is analogous to the construction of
\underline{{\rm Rep}}(S_{t})
in
[
D2
]
). The objects in this skeletal subcategory are of the form
[k]
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and should be thought of as the
k
-th tensor powers of the generating object
\mathbf{V}_{t}:=[1]
. We assign to every subspace
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k}
a morphism
[l]\to[k]
. The Hom space
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})}([l],[k])
is given by the span of the set

\{f_{R}:R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k}\;\text{ linear subspace }\}

and the composition of morphisms of the form
f_{R}
depends polynomially on a fixed parameter
t\in\mathbb{C}
. The rule
[k]\otimes[l]=[k+l]
turns this skeletal subcategory into a symmetric monoidal category. Its additive Karoubi envelope is baptized
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
; this idea is essentially the same as in Knop’s construction from
[
K
]
, and we show in Section
8.2
that the categories obtained in both constructions are equivalent.

One major difficulty of the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
-case compared to the
S_{t}
-case is to obtain a more explicit description of the morphism spaces
\operatorname{Hom}([k],[s])
. The first crucial observation is that the generating object
[1]
is an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
; this roughly means that
[1]
carries simultaneously a commutative Frobenius algebra structure and an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-module structure with some obvious compatibility conditions (see Definition
5.1.1
).

Let us explain the origin of this structure.
Given a symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{T}
, one may consider the category of commutative algebra objects in
\mathcal{T}
, and its opposite: the category of affine schemes in
\mathcal{T}
.

The categories
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
are “generated” by the finite
affine
scheme
X_{n}:={\mathbb{F}}^{n}_{q}
on which
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
acts. It is
an affine scheme
in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, and the standard representation
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
of
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
is isomorphic to
O(X_{n})^{*}
. Furthermore, the
affine
scheme
X_{n}
has an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear structure, which induces a corresponding
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear structure structure on
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
, compatible with the coalgebra structure on
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}=O(X_{n})^{*}
. Together, these structures make the representation
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
into an
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
. We show that the category
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is generated by this space, and extend this statement to the Deligne categories
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
.

In order to show this we first describe Hom spaces in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
by generators. By Corollary
4.0.2
the category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
interpolates the finite-dimensional complex representations of
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
in the sense that for values
t=q^{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we have a full, essentially surjective SM functor

F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

Since the functor
F_{n}
is fully faithful on small tensor powers, we may check the relations for morphisms in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
by looking at their image under the functor
F_{n}
.

The main result of this article is that the category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
satisfies the following universal property:

Theorem 1.1.1
.

(see Theorem
8.1.1
)
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Karoubi additive rigid SM category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
. Let
t=\dim(\mathbf{V})
. Then there exists a SM functor

F_{\mathbf{V}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C},\;\;\mathbf{V}_{t}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

Let us give some details on the construction
of
such a functor. First of all, we need to send
[k]
to
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
. We then need to know where to send morphisms
[k]\to[s]
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
.

Let us discuss the example
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
with its
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius algebra
\mathbf{V}_{n}=\mathbb{C}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
first. To any
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
we associate a morphism
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
.
We show that the set
\{f_{R}|R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
spans the space
\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
(see Lemma
3.1.2
) and is a basis if
n\geq s+k
. It is then easy to see (see Corollary
4.0.2
) that for
t=q^{n}
the functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
agrees with the specialization functor
F_{n}
from above when
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}_{n}\in\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
.

For a general
\mathcal{C}
we define morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}
,
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
which serve as analogues of the maps
f_{R}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
described above. We then prove that the morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}\in\mathcal{C}
satisfy the same relations on their compositions and tensor products as their counterparts in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. This allows us to construct a symmetric monoidal functor from the Deligne category into
\mathcal{C}
.

We also state other related universal properties, e.g. for
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
itself. As a Corollary we obtain:

Corollary 1.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
annihilated by some exterior power. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\dim\mathbf{V}=q^{n}
. Then the SM functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C}
seen in
8.1.1
factors through the functor
F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, and we have a natural SM isomorphism

F_{\mathbf{V}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\mathbf{V}}\circ F_{n}.

In a subsequent article, we will show that for a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
, and an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathcal{C}

not
annihilated by any exterior power, the functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}
factors through a certain tensor category
\mathrm{Rep}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{ab}}}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
sits as a full SM subcategory. This category
\mathrm{Rep}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{ab}}}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is called the
abelian envelope
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. We expect that this abelian envelope is related to the category of
VI
-modules of Putman and Sam (see
[
PS
]
) and algebraic representations
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
of
GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(cf.
[
N2
]
) similarly to the situation for symmetric groups (see
[
BEH
]
). The abelian envelope of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
in the sense of
[
EHS
]
has also been constructed in recent work of Harman-Snowden (see
[
HS
]
).

In this article we establish a first link between algebraic representations of the group
GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(as defined in Section
9.2.1
) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. Let
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
denote the full subcategory of
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
consisting of tensor powers of
{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\infty}
. We establish in Proposition
9.2.7
that there exists a (non-full) symmetric monoidal embedding of
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
into
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. The image of this embedding is called
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
; it contains all objects
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
as well as linear combinations of morphisms
f_{R}:[k]\to[s]
,
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
such that the projection
R\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
is onto. Both
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
and
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
do not possess a good notion of duality anymore. In particular, the object
{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\infty}\in\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
is no longer an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space. We therefore define in Definition
9.3.1
the weaker notion of an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space which is essentially an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space which does not necessarily possess a unit (and thus might not
be a
rigid
object
). An example of this notion is given by the object
{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\infty}\in\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, and by any
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space.

Theorem 1.1.3
.

(see Theorem
9.3.3
)
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
. Then there exists a symmetric monoidal
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear functor

\Gamma_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{C},\;\;[1]\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

The proof is very similar to the proof of Theorem
1.1.1
. The main difficulty lies in the construction of analogues of the morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}
in this context.

1.2.
Structure of the paper

In Section
3
we define the spanning set
\{f_{R}|R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
for
\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
, and show in Section
3.2
that the composition of two morphisms from this set is up to scalar again a morphism in this set. We also determine, in Section
3.4
, a set of generators for the morphisms in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
.

In Section
4
, we give an explicit construction of the Deligne categories
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
,
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
and show that for
t=q^{n}
there exists an essentially surjective, full symmetric monoidal functor
F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
.

In Section
5
we define
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius spaces as Frobenius algebra objects in a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear rigid symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with certain maps which essentially describe an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear structure on these objects; we show that the objects
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\in\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
and
[1]\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
are
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius spaces, and in Section
5.2
we introduce string diagrams which are a useful visual tool to manage morphisms between tensor powers of an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space. We also construct important examples of such morphisms: namely, given such a space
\mathbf{V}
in a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear symmetric monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
, we define for any matrix
A\in Mat_{s\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
a map
\mu_{A}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}
.

In Section
6
, we study the maps
\mu_{A}
and show that their compositions and tensor products can be conveniently described using linear algebra over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

In Section
7
we define morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}
,
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
in
\mathcal{C}
, which will serve as analogues of the maps
f_{R}
defined in Sections
3
,
4
. We will then prove that they satisfy the same relations on their compositions and tensor products as their counterparts in Section
4
.

In Section
8
we use the results on the morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}
to construct a SM functor from the Deligne category into a category
\mathcal{C}
which contains a
t
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space. Any
t
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
gives rise to such a functor. The representation category
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
satisfies a similar universal property with respect to
q^{n}
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius spaces
\mathbf{V}
annihilated by some exterior power.

In Section
9
we study the category
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, the full subcategory of
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})-mod
whose objects are tensor powers of
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}={\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}
where
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}=\bigcup_{n>0}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
. The representation
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space, and we show that
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
satisfies a universal property with respect to
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius spaces. For this we need analogs of the morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}
. These morphisms
\hat{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
are then defined and studied in Section
9.4
.

2.
Notation

Our main base field throughout the paper is
{\mathbb{C}}
, but since we work with complex representations of
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, we will also use vector spaces over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

All functors and all categories in this paper will be
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear.

2.1.
Finite linear groups

Let
q\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be a power of a prime.

To distinguish the vector spaces over the two fields, we denote by
{\dot{0}}
the zero vector in a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and by
0
the zero vector in a complex vector space.

Given a vector space
V
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, we denote by
v\dot{+}w\in V
the sum of vectors
v,w\in V
and by
\dot{a}v
multiplication of
v
by a scalar
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
; the operations over
{\mathbb{C}}
will be denoted by the usual notation. For larger sums, we denote by
\dot{\sum}
sums over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and by
\sum
sums over
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 2.1.1
.

•

We denote by
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
the group of all invertible
n\times n
-
matrices over the finite field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

•

Let
A\in Mat_{d\times m}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. We denote by
Row(A)\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{m}
the span of rows of
A
.

•

For any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
, write
\vec{v}=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\\
\vdots\\
v_{s}\end{bmatrix}\in\left({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\right)^{\oplus s}
. For any matrix
M\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
we can now define
M\vec{v}
as an element of
\left({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\right)^{\oplus k}
(we will write it again as a column vector).

•

We will denote
\mathbf{V}_{n}:={\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
.

•

I_{d}
stands for the identity matrix of size
d\times d
over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

2.2.
SM categories and SM functors

Let
k
be any field. We adopt the same notion of a
k
-linear symmetric monoidal (SM) category as
[
DM
]
. In particular we have a binatural family of braiding morphisms
\gamma_{XY}:X\otimes Y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}Y\otimes X
which satisfy the constraints of
[
DM
]
. For an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
, let
X^{*}
denotes its dual (if it exists). If every object has a dual, we call
\mathcal{C}

rigid
.

A
k
-linear functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
between two SM categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is symmetric monoidal (
SM
for short) if it is equipped with natural isomorphisms
c_{XY}^{F}:F(X)\otimes F(Y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}F(X\otimes Y)
and
{\mathbf{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}F({\mathbf{1}})
satisfying the usual compatibility conditions
[
DM
, Definition 1.8]
. If
F
is a monoidal functor and
X^{*}
the dual of
X
, then
F(X^{\ast})
is a dual of
F(X)
.

We say that a
k
-linear SM category
\mathcal{C}
is a tensor category if

(i)

\mathcal{C}
is abelian;

(ii)

the canonical morphism
k\to\operatorname{End}({\mathbf{1}})
is an isomorphism;

(iii)

\mathcal{C}
is rigid;

(iv)

every object in
\mathcal{C}
has finite length.

We also call such categories ‘pre-Tannakian categories’ as in
[
CO2
]
. If such a category has in addition a faithful
k
-linear tensor functor to
Vec_{k}
, the category of finite dimensional
k
vector spaces, we will call it Tannakian.

3.
Endomorphisms of
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}

3.1.
Basis of endomorphisms

Fix
n\geq 1
. Let
G=GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
,
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
(seen as a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
).

Let
s,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
.
Recall that

\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}={\mathbb{C}}V^{\times s}.

We will consider a natural basis for
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}
given by the elements of
V^{\times s}
. For
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in V
, we write
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s})\in V^{\times s}
.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
be a linear
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-subspace. We define a
G
-invariant subspace

R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}=\{(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s+k})\in V^{\times(s+k)}\;\rvert\;\forall u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{s+k})\in R,\;\dot{\sum}_{i}u_{i}v_{i}=0\}

in
V^{\times(s+k)}
. This allows us to define a map

f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}\\
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\end{subarray}}w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}.

Example 3.1.1
.

(1)

For
R=\{{\dot{0}}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
we have:

f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\mapsto\sum_{(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}}w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}.

For instance, for
s=k=1
, we obtain:
f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
v\mapsto\sum_{w\in V}w
for any
v\in V
.

(2)

For
R={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
we have:

f_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\mapsto\begin{cases}{\dot{0}}\otimes\ldots\otimes{\dot{0}}&\text{ if }\;v_{1}=\ldots=v_{s}={\dot{0}}\\
0&\text{ else }\end{cases}.

For instance, for
s=0
,
k=1
, we obtain:
f_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{1}}:{\mathbb{C}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
1\mapsto{\dot{0}}
.

(3)

For
s=k=1
and
R=span_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(1,0)
we obtain the map
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
v\to{\dot{0}}
for any
v\in V\setminus\{{\dot{0}}\}
, and
{\dot{0}}\mapsto\sum_{w\in V}w
.

(4)

For
s=k=1
and
b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, let
R=span_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(-b,1)
. We obtain the map
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
v\to\dot{b}v
for any
v\in V
.

Lemma 3.1.2
.

The set
\{f_{R}|R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
spans the space
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
. Furthermore, if
n\geq s+k
then this set is a basis of
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
.

Proof.

The space
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
has a basis
\{\breve{f}_{O}|O\in V^{\times(s+k)}//G\}
which is parameterized by the orbits of
G
on
V^{\times s}\times V^{\times k}=V^{\times(s+k)}
. Here
\breve{f}_{O}
is defined as follows: for
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s})\in V^{\times s}
,

\breve{f}_{O}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}\\
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in O\end{subarray}}w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}.

The orbits
O\in V^{\times(s+k)}//G
are in bijection with subspaces
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
. This bijection can be constructed as follows: a subspace
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
such that
\dim R\geq s+k-n
corresponds to the orbit

O_{R}:=\{(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\,\rvert\;\dim span\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s},w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}=s+k-\dim R\}

If
\dim R<s+k-n
, let us write
O_{R}:=\emptyset
. Thus
\{\breve{f}_{O_{R}}|\;R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
is a spanning set of
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
, and it is a basis if
s+k\leq n
.

Note that for any
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
, we have:

\dim span\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s},w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}\leq s+k-\dim R.

Hence

f_{R}=\sum_{R\subset S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}}\breve{f}_{O_{S}}

and so
\{f_{R}|\;R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
is a spanning set of
\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
, and it is a basis if
s+k\leq n
.
∎

3.2.
Composition

Let
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
,
f_{S}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}
be two endomorphisms as above corresponding to subspaces
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k},S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
.

Consider the subspaces
R\times\{\vec{0}\},\{\vec{0}\}\times S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
. We will denote them
(R,0)
and
(0,S)
.

We will also denote by
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})
the image of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
: namely, this is the subspace of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
consisting of vectors whose coordinates
l+1,\ldots,l+k
are zero.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Denote

S\circledast R:=\left((R,0)+(0,S)\right)\cap({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}),

and denote by
S\star R
the (isomorphic) image of
S\circledast R
under the identification
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+s}\cong({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})
. Let

d(R,S)=k-\dim\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ projection of }(R,0)+(0,S)\\
\text{ on the subspace }{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\end{subarray}\right)

where the subspace
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
consists of the vectors in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
whose coordinates
1,\ldots,l
, and
l+k+1,\ldots,l+k+s
are zero.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

The composition
f_{S}\circ f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}
equals

q^{n\cdot d(R,S)}f_{S\star R}.

Proof.

Let
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in V
. We have:

(f_{S}\circ f_{R})(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in V^{\times l},\;\;(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}\\
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\,\text{ and }\\
(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k}|u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in S^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\end{subarray}}u_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes u_{l}

Clearly any summand in this sum satisfies:

(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k}|u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in(R,0)^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\cap(0,S)^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}=\left((R,0)+(0,S)\right)^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}

for some
(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}
and so

(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in(S\star R)^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
. Vice versa, for every
(u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in V^{\times l}
such that
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in(S\star R)^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
, we have: the term
u_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes u_{l}
appears in the sum above with multiplicity which is equal to the number of elements in the set

Z=\{(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}\;\rvert\;(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\,\text{ and }\\
(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k}|u_{1}|\ldots|u_{l})\in S^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\}

The set
Z\subset V^{\times k}
is thus an affine
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-space, described by a system of linear equations over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
with variables
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\in V
. The rank of this system is precisely the dimension of the projection of
(R,0)+(0,S)
on the subspace
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k+l}
, that is,
k-d(R,S)
. Then the size of
Z
is
\left|{V}\right|^{d(R,S)}=q^{nd(R,S)}
which completes the proof of the statement.

∎

3.3.
Orthogonal indexing

In
[
K
,
K2
]
, there appears another indexing of the basis
\{f_{R}:R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\}
for the
Hom
-space
Hom_{GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
: namely, the morphism which we denoted by
f_{R}
would correspond in the notation of
[
K
,
K2
]
to the subspace

(1)

\{(v_{1},\ldots,v_{s+k})\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\;\rvert\;\forall u=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{s+k})\in R,\;\dot{\sum}_{i}u_{i}v_{i}=0\}.

The goal of this subsection is to establish the compatibility between the bases of morphisms used in this paper and the bases used in
[
K
,
K2
]
.

In this Subsection we will denote the subspace (
1
) of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
by
R^{\perp}

(so that
R^{\perp}:=R^{\perp}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{1}}
)
. Note that for any
R,S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
, we have:

(R^{\perp})^{\perp}=R,\;\;(R+S)^{\perp}=R^{\perp}\cap S^{\perp}.

Remark 3.3.1
.

Considering the isomorphism
V^{\times(s+k)}=V\otimes_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
, the subspace
R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\subset V^{\times(s+k)}
we considered before would be given by
V\otimes_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}R^{\perp}
.

To see
what
the composition of morphisms
looks like
in the alternative indexing, we will use the following construction.

Let
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
,
f_{S}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}
correspond to subspaces
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k},S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{{R^{\perp}\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S^{\perp}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\perp}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}}

where the bottom arrows are compositions of embeddings
R^{\perp}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k},S^{\perp}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
with the projections on the subspaces
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
.

Notation 3.3.2
.

We denote by
S^{\perp}\diamond R^{\perp}
the image of the natural map
R^{\perp}\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S^{\perp}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
, and by
e(S^{\perp},R^{\perp})
the dimension of the kernel of this map.

Lemma 3.3.3
.

We have:
S^{\perp}\diamond R^{\perp}=(S\star R)^{\perp}
and
e(S^{\perp},R^{\perp})=d(R,S)
.

Proof.

Consider the subspace
S\circledast R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
we defined in Section
3.2
. Then

(S\circledast R)^{\perp}=\left((R,0)+(0,S)\right)^{\perp}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})}}^{\perp}=(R,0)^{\perp}\cap(0,S)^{\perp}+(0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},0).

Let us use this equality to describe
(S\star R)^{\perp}
. Under the indentification
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
, the space
(S\star R)^{\perp}
is identified with the subspace of
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})
which is orthogonal to
S\circledast R
, so it is given by the intersection

(S\circledast R)^{\perp}\cap({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})=(R,0)^{\perp}\cap(0,S)^{\perp}\cap({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}).

Thus

(S\star R)^{\perp}=\{(v,u)\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}\;|\;\exists w\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\,:\,(v,w)\in R^{\perp},\,(w,u)\in S^{\perp}\}.

On the other hand, the map
R^{\perp}\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S^{\perp}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
is the composition of the embedding

\iota:R^{\perp}\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S^{\perp}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s},\;\;((v,w),(w,u))\longmapsto(v,w,u)

and the projection
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
. This proves that
S^{\perp}\diamond R^{\perp}=(S\star R)^{\perp}
.

Now,
e(S^{\perp},R^{\perp})
equals

\dim Ker\left(R^{\perp}\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S^{\perp}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\times{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}\right)=\dim\left(Im(\iota)\cap(0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},0)\right)=\dim\left((R,0)^{\perp}\cap(0,S)^{\perp}\cap(0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},0)\right),

while

d(R,S)=k-\dim\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ projection of }(R,0)+(0,S)\\
\text{ on the subspace }{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\end{subarray}\right)=\dim Coker\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ projection of }(R,0)+(0,S)\\
\text{ on the subspace }{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\end{subarray}\right).

Denote:
U:=(R,0)+(0,S)
,
W:=({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})
subspaces of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}
. Then
W^{\perp}=(0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k},0)
. Let
p:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\to W^{\perp}
denote the natural projection.

We have:
e(S^{\perp},R^{\perp})=\dim U^{\perp}\cap W^{\perp}
, while

\displaystyle d(R,S)

\displaystyle=\dim Coker(p|_{U})=\dim W^{\perp}-\dim Im(p|_{U})=\dim W^{\perp}-\dim U+\dim Ker(p|_{U})=

\displaystyle=\dim W^{\perp}-\dim U+\dim U\cap W=\dim W^{\perp}-\dim U^{\perp}\cap W

\displaystyle=\dim U^{\perp}\cap W^{\perp}=e(S^{\perp},R^{\perp}).

∎

3.4.
Generating morphisms

We will now give a list of generating morphisms in the full subcategory
\mathcal{T}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
of
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
generated by tensor powers of
\mathbf{V}_{n}
.

The following proposition will follow from the results of Section
8
. Namely, we will construct a Deligne category which will allow
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
as a quotient, and show that the analogues of the maps below generate the morphisms in the Deligne category.

However, for motivational purposes, we include here a direct proof of the proposition.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

Consider the following morphisms:

(1)

Morphisms

\displaystyle\varepsilon:=f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;,1\mapsto\sum_{v\in V}v,\;\;\varepsilon^{*}:=f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to{\mathbbm{1}},\;\;,v\to 1\;\;\text{ for }v\in V,

\displaystyle m:=f_{\{(a+b,-a,-b)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v\otimes w\mapsto\delta_{v,w}v\;\;\text{ for }v,w\in V,

\displaystyle m^{*}:=f_{\{(a+b,-a,-b)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v\mapsto v\otimes v\;\;\text{ for }v\in V,

\displaystyle\sigma:=f_{\{(a,b,-b,-a)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v\otimes w\mapsto w\otimes v\;\;\text{ for }v,w\in V.

(2)

Morphisms

\displaystyle z:=f_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{1}}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;1\mapsto{\dot{0}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q},\;\mu_{a}:=f_{\{(-ab,b)|b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v\mapsto\dot{a}v\;\;\text{ for }v\in V,

\displaystyle\dot{+}:=f_{\{(b,b,-b)|b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v\otimes w\mapsto v\dot{+}w\;\;\text{ for }v,w\in V.

These morphisms generate the space
\left(\bigoplus_{k,l\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{G}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}),\otimes,\circ\right)
.

The morphisms described in Proposition
3.4.1
can be interpreted as follows:

•

The morphisms
\mu_{a},\dot{+}
give
\mathbf{V}_{n}
the structure of an (infinite) dimensional vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, with
z:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
defining the zero vector
{\dot{0}}\in\mathbf{V}_{n}
;

•

The rest of the morphisms make
\mathbf{V}_{n}
into a commutative Frobenius algebra in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
. The algebra is self-dual, via the pairings

\displaystyle ev:=f_{\{(a,-a)|a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n}\to{\mathbbm{1}},\;\;v\otimes w\mapsto\delta_{v,w}\;\;\text{ for }v,w\in V,

\displaystyle coev:=f_{\{(a,-a)|a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;1\mapsto\sum_{v\in V}v\otimes v.

where

ev=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m,\;\;coev=m^{*}\circ\varepsilon.

In particular, we may replace

m,\varepsilon
with
coev,ev
in the list above. Indeed,

m=(ev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes m^{*}),\;\;\varepsilon=(\varepsilon^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ coev.

Remark 3.4.2
.

We have:
\mu_{0}=z\circ\varepsilon^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+l}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-subspace. We will show that
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}
can be obtained from the morphisms
listed in Proposition
3.4.1
using tensor products and
composition.

We already saw that the morphisms
ev,coev
can be obtained
from the generating morphisms.

Using the maps
\sigma
,
ev
and
coev
, we can reduce the question to the case when

f_{R}
sends

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}
(
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
) to the tensor
product of
l
vectors

\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{j1}v_{j}\right)\otimes\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{j2}v_{j}\right)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{jl}v_{j}\right).

for some fixed
A\in Mat_{k\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

In other words,

R=\{(Au,-u)|u\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l}\}
for some
A\in Mat_{k\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

If
l=0
, then
f_{R}=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes k}
and we are done.

If
k=0
, then
f_{R}=z^{\otimes l}
and we are done.
So from now on we assume that

l,k\neq 0
.

Next, we can present our morphism
f_{R}
as composition

f_{R}=\left(\otimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{A_{i}}\right)\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}

where

•

\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}
is given by

\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto v_{1}^{\otimes l}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}^{\otimes l},\ \text{ for }v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V

•

w:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl}
is an endomorphism given by
a permutation of the tensor factors,
so that
w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}
is the morphism

\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k})^{\otimes l},\ \text{ for }v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V.

Given a pure tensor
v_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{kl-1}
,
w
sends it to a pure tensor
v^{\prime}_{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes v^{\prime}_{kl-1}
such that
v^{\prime}_{r+qk}:=v_{q+rl}
for
0\leq q<l
,
0\leq r<k
.

•

\mu_{A_{i}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
is given by

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto\dot{\sum}_{j}\dot{A}_{ji}v_{j}.

Clearly,
\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}
is obtained from
m^{*}
through tensor products and
iterated compositions, while
w
is obtained from
\sigma
through tensor products
(with
\operatorname{Id}
) and compositions. So it remains to check that for each
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}]\in Mat_{1\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the map

\mu_{[a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}]}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{a}_{j}v_{j}

can be obtained from the morphisms listed in Proposition

3.4.1
. Indeed, this is done by composing the map

\otimes_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{a_{j}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\mapsto\dot{a}_{1}v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\dot{a}_{k}v_{k}

with iterative applications of the map

\dot{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s-1},\;\;v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\mapsto(v_{1}\dot{+}v_{2})\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}

for
s=k,k-1,\ldots,2
. This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

4.
Construction of the Karoubian Deligne category

Fix
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
. We construct the Deligne category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
in this section, the analogue of
\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(S_{t})
. The original construction is due to Knop (
[
K
,
K2
]
), but his definition is a bit different and we only show in Section
8.2
that our categories are equivalent. It also appears in work of Meir (
[
M
]
) (although there only the semisimplified version is considered) and has
recently been studied
by Harman and Snowden
[
HS
]
.

We define
\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
as a category whose objects are non-negative integers, denoted by
[k]
.
Define

Rel_{s,k}=\{R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\;\text{ linear subspace }\},\quad\;\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})}([s],[k])={\mathbb{C}}Rel_{s,k}

with composition of morphisms given as follows: for
R\in Rel_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel_{k,l}
we set

S\circ R:=t^{d(R,S)}S\star R.

We will also define the tensor structure of
\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
by setting
[l]\otimes[k]:=[l+k]
, and tensor products of morphisms are given as follows: for
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}}
and
R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}}
, set
f_{R_{1}}\otimes f_{R_{2}}:=f_{R_{1}\times R_{2}}
where
R_{1}\times R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
is considered as an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspace.

Each object is then self-dual via the evaluation and coevaluation maps

f_{R}:[k]\otimes[k]\to{\mathbbm{1}},\;\;f_{R}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to[k]\otimes[k]

where
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k}
is given by
R=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},-a_{k},\ldots,-a_{1})|a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}.

The symmetric structure is given by the morphisms

\sigma=f_{R^{\prime}}:[l]\otimes[k]\to[k]\otimes[l]
where

R^{\prime}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},-b_{1},\ldots,-b_{k},-a_{1},\ldots,-a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k+2l}

It is easy to see that we obtain a rigid symmetric monoidal category in this way.

We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
as the Karoubi additive envelope of
\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
. The reasons for this notation will only become fully clear in an upcoming article. In the
S_{t}
-case the category
\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(S_{t})
embeds into a tensor category
\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}^{ab}(S_{t})
, the abelian envelope
[
CO
]
. The original Deligne category then coincides with the full subcategory of tilting objects in
\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(S_{t})
. We expect the same statement in the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
-case.

The following result is a straightforward corollary of the results in Section
3
:

Proposition 4.0.1
.

For values
t=q^{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we have a full SM functor

F_{n}:\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))

sending the object
[k]
to
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
and sending the morphism
f_{R}:[l]\to[k]
to the morphism
f_{R}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
as defined in Section
3
.

This functor extends naturally to the Karoubi additive envelope
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
of
\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
, and we obtain:

Corollary 4.0.2
.

For values
t=q^{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we have a full, essentially surjective SM functor

F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})).

Proof.

To show that this functor is essentially surjective, recall the category
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is generated by the objects
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
,
k\geq 0
under taking finite direct sums and direct summands.
∎

Remark 4.0.3
.

By Lemma
3.1.2
, the functor
F_{n}
is fully faithful on the full subcategory
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}^{\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
generated by the objects
[k]
where
0\leq k\leq\lfloor n/2\rfloor
. Analogous results in the
S_{t}
,
O_{t}
and
GL_{t}
-case were proven in
[
D2
]
.

5.
Frobenius linear spaces

Throughout this section, let
\mathcal{C}
be a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear rigid SM category. We introduce the notion of a Frobenius linear space which is required to state the universal property of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. The crucial assertions for the proof are Lemma
7.0.11
and Proposition
7.0.12
.

5.1.
Frobenius linear spaces: definition

Definition 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{C}
be an object equipped with the following
structures:

(
Str_{1}
)

\mathbf{V}
is equipped with the structure of a Frobenius algebra object in

\mathcal{C}
. That is,
\mathbf{V}
is equipped with maps
m:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbf{V}
,
\varepsilon:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}
,
m^{*}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}
,
\varepsilon^{*}:\mathbf{V}\to{\mathbbm{1}}
such that the following conditions hold:

(
Fr_{1}
)

\mathbf{V}
is a commutative unital algebra object with
multiplication
m
and unit
\varepsilon
, and a
cocommutative counital coalgebra object
with comultiplication
m^{*}
and
counit
\varepsilon^{*}
,

(
Fr_{2}
)

Frobenius Relations:
m^{*}\circ m=(\operatorname{Id}\otimes m)\circ(m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id})=(m\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes m^{*})
, and

Speciality Relation:
m\circ m^{*}=\operatorname{Id}
.

(
Str_{2}
)

\mathbf{V}
is a module over the field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. In other words,
\mathbf{V}
is equipped
with
maps

\displaystyle\dot{+}:\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V},

\displaystyle\mu:({\mathbb{F}}_{q},\cdot)\to(End_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}),\circ),\;\;a\mapsto\mu_{a},

\displaystyle z:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}

satisfying the following conditions:

(
Lin_{1}
)

\dot{+}
is associative and commutative:
\dot{+}\circ(\dot{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id})=\dot{+}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\dot{+})
,
\dot{+}\circ\sigma=\dot{+}
where
\sigma\in\operatorname{End}(\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V})
is the symmetry morphism.

(
Lin_{2}
)

z
serves as “the embedding of the
\dot{0}
vector”:
\dot{+}\circ(z\otimes\operatorname{Id})=\operatorname{Id},\dot{+}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z)=\operatorname{Id}
,

(
Lin_{3}
)

Properties of the maps
\mu_{a}
: for all
a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
\mu_{a}\circ\mu_{b}=\mu_{ab}
and
\mu_{1}=\operatorname{Id}
,
\mu_{0}=z\circ\varepsilon^{*}
.

(
Lin_{4}
)

Linearity of
\mu_{a}
with respect to

\dot{+}
: for any
a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,

\mu_{a+b}=\dot{+}\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{b})\circ m^{*}
.

Distributivity of
\mu_{a}
: for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
,
\mu_{a}\circ\dot{+}=\dot{+}\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})
.

Assume furthermore that the above structures satisfy the following
compatibility relations:

(
Rel_{1}
)

For each
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
,
\mu_{a}
is a morphism of coalgebras, i.e. the
following diagrams are commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{a}}
\scriptstyle{m^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbbm{1}}}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{*}}

Furthermore,
\mu_{a}
respects the multiplication structure, i.e. the
following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a}}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{a}}

(
Rel_{2}
)

The map
z
is a morphism of coalgebras, i.e. the
following diagrams are commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}
\textstyle{{\mathbbm{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbbm{1}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z\otimes z}

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbbm{1}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbbm{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}

Furthermore,
z
respects the multiplication structure, i.e. the
following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbbm{1}}\otimes{\mathbbm{1}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z\otimes z}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}}
\textstyle{{\mathbbm{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{z}

(
Rel_{3}
)

The morphism
\dot{+}
is a morphism of coalgebras, i.e. the
following diagrams are commutative:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{+}}
\scriptstyle{(\operatorname{Id}\circ\sigma\circ\operatorname{Id})\circ(m^{*}\otimes m^{*})}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{+}\otimes\dot{+}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}}

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{*}}
\textstyle{{{\mathbbm{1}}}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{+}}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon^{*}\otimes\varepsilon^{*}}

Here
\sigma:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}
is the symmetry morphism.

(
Rel_{4}
)

Cancellation Axiom:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}\otimes m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 4}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{+}\otimes\dot{+}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Id}\otimes m}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\dot{+}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}}

Such an object
\mathbf{V}
will be called
an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Example 5.1.2
.

We have seen in Section
3
that the object
\mathbf{V}_{n}
in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is equipped with the required morphisms
(m,\varepsilon,m^{*},\varepsilon^{*},\dot{+},\mu,z)
, and it is of dimension
q^{n}
. We have already seen that these satisfy the Frobenius algebra relations
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)(

⁢
F

r
1

)
,
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)(

⁢
F

r
2

)
and it is straightforward that the relations
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)
(“being a module over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
”) are satisfied as well.

The compatibility relations state that the Frobenius algebra structure is compatible with the
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-module structure on
\mathbf{V}_{n}
.

Intuitively, the morphisms
(m,\varepsilon,m^{*},\varepsilon^{*})
serve as logical tools, allowing us to “see” the vectors
v\in V
in the space
\mathbf{V}_{n}
and to write down “equations” with these vectors
v\in V
.
The map
\varepsilon^{*}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to{\mathbb{C}}
which sends each
v\in V
to
1
serves to “verify” whether a given vector belongs to
V
, the map
m
serves to “compare” vectors
v,w\in V
and check whether they are equal, and the map
m^{*}
which allows us to “double” a given vector
v\in V
in order to be used multiple times in an equation.

Let us illustrate the compatibility relations in this case.
Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
translates to:

\displaystyle\forall v,w\in V,\forall a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times},\;\;

\displaystyle\left((\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})\circ m^{*}\right)(v)=\dot{a}v\otimes\dot{a}v=m^{*}(\dot{a}v),\;\;\varepsilon^{*}(\dot{a}v)=1,

\displaystyle m(\dot{a}v\otimes\dot{a}w)=\delta_{\dot{a}v,\dot{a}w}\dot{a}v=\delta_{v,w}\dot{a}v=\dot{a}m(v\otimes w).

Intuitively, these conditions state that
\dot{a}v\in V
, that
\dot{a}v=\dot{a}w\;\Leftrightarrow\;v=w
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
, and that
\dot{a}v\otimes\dot{a}v
is both a “doubling” of the vector
\dot{a}v\in V
and a multiplication of two copies of
v
by the same scalar
a
.

Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
2

)
translates to:

\displaystyle m^{*}(\dot{0})=\dot{0}\otimes\dot{0}=(z^{*}\otimes z^{*})(1),\;\;\varepsilon^{*}(\dot{0})=1,

\displaystyle m(\dot{0}\otimes\dot{0})=\dot{0}=z^{*}(1).

In other words, this condition states that
\dot{0}\in V
, that
\dot{0}=\dot{0}
and that “doubling” the vector
\dot{0}\in V
is the same as taking the image of
z^{*}\otimes z^{*}
.

Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
3

)
translates to the following: for any
v,w\in V
, applying the map
\left(\dot{+}\otimes\dot{+}\right)\left(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Id}\right)\left(m^{*}\otimes m^{*}\right)
to
v\otimes w
gives
(v\dot{+}w)\otimes(v\dot{+}w)
, which is also
m^{*}(v\dot{+}w)
. Intuitively, this means that “doubling”
v\dot{+}w
is the same as “doubling”
v
, “doubling”
w
and then adding the copies separately.

The second part of the condition just states that

\displaystyle\forall v,w\in V,\;\;

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{*}(v)\otimes\varepsilon^{*}(w)=1=\varepsilon^{*}(v\dot{+}w),

which intuitively means that for
v,w\in V
,
v\dot{+}w\in V
as well.

Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
4

)
(“Cancellation Axiom”) translates to:

\displaystyle\forall v,w,u\in V,\;\;\left(m\circ\left(\dot{+}\otimes\dot{+}\right)\right)(v\otimes u\otimes u\otimes w)=\delta_{v\dot{+}u,u\dot{+}w}(v\dot{+}u)=\dot{+}(u\otimes\delta_{v,w}v)

In other words, in this setting the Cancellation Axiom is equivalent to the statement

\displaystyle\forall v,w,u\in V,\;\;v=w\Leftrightarrow v\dot{+}u=w\dot{+}u,

which justifies its name (see also Lemma
6.3.1
).

The following is the main example we will consider:

Example 5.1.3
.

Let
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
.
Consider the object
[1]
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
; by abuse of notation, we will denote it by
\mathbf{V}_{t}
, and often write
\mathbf{V}_{t}^{\otimes k}
instead of
[k]
.

Just like we proved in Proposition
3.4.1
, the following
morphisms generate the space
\left(\bigoplus_{m,k\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}}}}(\mathbf{V}_{t}^{\otimes m},\mathbf{V}_{t}^{\otimes k}),\otimes,\circ\right)
:

(1)

Morphisms

\displaystyle\varepsilon:=f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{t},\;\;

\displaystyle m:=f_{\{(a+b,-a,-b)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{t}\to\mathbf{V}_{t}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{*}:=f_{\{{\dot{0}}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\to{\mathbbm{1}},\;\;

\displaystyle m^{*}:=f_{\{(a+b,-a,-b)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\to\mathbf{V}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{t}

\displaystyle\sigma:=f_{\{(a,b,-b,-a)|a,b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{t}\to\mathbf{V}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{t}

(2)

Morphisms

\displaystyle z:=f_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{1}}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}_{t},\;\;1\mapsto{\dot{0}},

\displaystyle\forall a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q},\;\mu_{a}:=f_{\{(-ab,b)|b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\to\mathbf{V}_{t},\;\;

\displaystyle\dot{+}:=f_{\{(b,b,-b)|b\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}}:\mathbf{V}_{t}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{t}\to\mathbf{V}_{t}.

To show that these maps satisfy the conditions of Definition
5.1.1
,
we first note that in the setting of the category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
, the required relations on the morphisms
\varepsilon,m,\varepsilon^{*},m^{*},\sigma,z,\mu_{a},\dot{+}
are equivalent to the condition that certain polynomials in
t
are constant and equal to zero. Therefore it is enough to establish that the required relations hold for an infinite set of values of
t
: for example, for
t=q^{2n}
where
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.

Recall that by Remark
4.0.3
, for
t=q^{2n}
, the functor
F_{2n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{2n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is fully faithful on the full subcategory
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}^{\leq n}
generated by the objects
[k]
,
0\leq k\leq n
. The morphisms described in Definition
5.1.1
belong to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}^{\leq 3}
. Thus it is enough to check that the relations in Definition
5.1.1
hold in
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{6}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, which we have seen already.

This makes
\mathbf{V}_{t}=[1]
an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space of categorical dimension

t
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
.

Remark 5.1.4
.

In
[
KS
]
, Khovanov and Sazdanovic constructed
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear Karoubi additive symmetric monoidal categories
Kob_{\alpha}
attached to any power series
\alpha
with coefficients
\alpha=(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots)
with
\alpha_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. These were later studied, under the name
DCob_{\alpha}
, by Khovanov, Ostrik and Kononov in
[
KOK
]
. The categories

DCob_{\alpha}
all contain a distinguished commutative Frobenius algebra which in some sense realizes the power series
\alpha
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
contains a distinguished commutative Frobenius algebra, it is tempting to ask whether it is of the form
DCob_{\alpha}
for some power series
\alpha
. This is not the case. Computing the power series
\alpha
for
[1]
and parameter
t=q^{n}
yields the constant power series
\alpha=(q^{n},q^{n},\ldots)
. In this case
DCob_{\alpha}\cong\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(S_{t=q^{n}})
. This result should be expected since the power series
\alpha
only depends on the Frobenius structure and not on the additional
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-module structure.

Remark 5.1.5
.

The relation
\varepsilon^{*}\circ z=\operatorname{Id}
appearing in
(

⁢
R
e

l
2

)
implies that
z^{*}\circ z=\operatorname{Id}
, so
{\mathbbm{1}}
is a direct summand of
\mathbf{V}
.
Indeed,

z^{*}\circ z=ev\circ(z\otimes z)=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m\circ(z\otimes z)=\varepsilon^{*}\circ z=\operatorname{Id}.

We now introduce some notation which will be used throughout the paper:

Notation 5.1.6
.

We denote:

\displaystyle ev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle=ev\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes ev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes ev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ\ldots

\displaystyle coev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}

\displaystyle=\ldots\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes coev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes coev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ coev

as well as the morphisms

\displaystyle\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}={ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes w),

\displaystyle\overline{coev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}=(\operatorname{Id}\otimes w)\circ{coev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}

where
w:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
stands for the composition of symmetry morphisms
\sigma
which corresponds to the longest element in the symmetric group
S_{k}
.

The morphisms
\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}
,
\overline{coev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}
make
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
self-dual.

Example 5.1.7
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, the map
ev_{\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}
takes
v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}
(where
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
) to
1
if
v_{1}=v_{2}=\ldots=v_{k}
, and to
0
otherwise.

Similarly, the map
coev_{\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
takes
v
to
v\otimes\ldots\otimes v
whenever
v\in V
.

5.2.
String diagrams

Let us consider an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

In order to study the homomorphisms
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
, we
will draw string diagrams to encode morphisms generated by the morphisms
m,\varepsilon,m^{*},\varepsilon^{*},\sigma,z,\dot{+}
and
\mu_{a}
(
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
). A morphism
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
for given
s,k\geq 0
will be drawn as a diagram located in a horizontal strip of the plane, connecting
s
endpoints (denoted by vertical strands) on the lower border of the strip with
k
endpoints on the upper border of the strip. The diagram consists of a collection of strings with decorations in the spirit of
[
S
]
.

We say that two diagrams are equivalent when they encode equal morphisms.

Composition corresponds to vertical concatenation of diagrams: given two diagrams
D
and
D^{\prime}
corresponding to morphisms
f:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and
f^{\prime}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
, we draw the diagram for
f^{\prime}\circ f
by stacking
D^{\prime}
on top of
D
and identifying the
k
upper endpoints of
D
with the
k
lower endpoints of
D^{\prime}
.

Drawing strings next to each other horizontally denotes the monoidal product, and having no strings at all denotes an endomorphism of
{\mathbbm{1}}
. Reflection with respect to a horizontal axis sends a morphism
f
to
f^{*}
due to the self-duality of
\mathbf{V}
(see Lemma
6.4.1
and also Remark
6.4.3
below).

The morphisms described in Definition

Here are some special morphisms and their string diagrams, which we will often use:

Definition 5.2.1

(Special string diagrams)
.

Let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius
space in
\mathcal{C}
. We will use the following notation for iterative
compositions: for
r,d\geq 2
,

\displaystyle(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes d},\;\;(m^{*})^{it}:=\ldots\circ(m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}})\circ(m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}})\circ m^{*}

\displaystyle(\dot{+})^{it}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}\to\mathbf{V},\;\;(\dot{+})^{it}:=\dot{+}\circ(\dot{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}})\circ(\dot{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}})\circ\ldots

For
k=d=1
, we will set
(m^{*})^{it}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}=(\dot{+})^{it}
.

Due to the coassociativity of
m^{*}
, we may draw
(m^{*})^{it}

as a diagram with
d
strands in the upper part:

\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

Due to the associativity of
\dot{+}
, draw
(\dot{+})^{it}

as a diagram with
r
strands in the lower part:

\dot{+}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

Let
A\in Mat_{r\times d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. We will denote by

\mu_{A}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes d}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}
the composition

\mathbf{V}^{\otimes d}\xrightarrow{\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes d}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes rd}\xrightarrow{w}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes rd}\xrightarrow{\bigotimes_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq d}\mu_{A_{i,j}}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes rd}\xrightarrow{\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes r}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}

where
w
is a permutation of factors as in the proof of Proposition

3.4.1
and
\bigotimes_{1\leq i\leq r,1\leq j\leq d}\mu_{A_{i,j}}
means that we multiply the
(i+d(j-1))
-th factor by
A_{i,j}

for each
i,j
. We will draw
\mu_{A}
diagramatically as follows:

A
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

A=[a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}]

by
A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{r}
, we have the following equality:

(2)

\hbox to43.08pt{\vbox to64.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 21.53957pt\lower-42.87914pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Here each intersection of the strands of the form
or
means that we apply the symmetry morphism
\sigma:\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}
on the relevant factors.

Example 5.2.2
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, and any matrix
A\in Mat_{r\times d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the operator
\mu_{A}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes d}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes r}
is given by

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{d}\longmapsto\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{d}\dot{A}_{1,j}v_{j}\right)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{d}\dot{A}_{r,j}v_{j}\right)\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes r}

for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{d}\in V
.

Let us give this map an interpretation in terms of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear vector spaces.

Recall that
V={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
denotes the basis of
\mathbf{V}_{n}
and write the elements of
V^{\times d}=V^{\dot{\oplus}d}
as column vectors whose entries are given by elements of
V
. Then
\mu_{A}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes d}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes r}
corresponds to the
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear operator

\displaystyle V^{\times d}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow V^{\times r},\;\;\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{d}\end{bmatrix}\longmapsto A\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{d}\end{bmatrix}.

Example 5.2.3
.

For
A=\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}\in Mat_{1\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the morphism
\mu_{A}
is just
(\dot{+})^{it}
. Similarly, for
B=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in Mat_{k\times 1}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the morphism
\mu_{B}
is just
(m^{*})^{it}
.

6.
Composing generating morphisms

In this section, we give some results concerning the morphisms from Definition
5.1.1
and the morphisms
\mu_{A}
for a general
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear rigid SM category
\mathcal{C}
and an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathcal{C}
. We will also provide examples for most of the statements in the case
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, using the notation of Section
3
.

The main purpose of this section is to prove the following statements, given in Propositions
6.2.10
and
6.1.2
: for any
B\in Mat_{s\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A\in Mat_{k\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{k^{\prime}\times l^{\prime}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, we have
\mu_{B}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{BA}
and
\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A}^{\prime}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}
; thus the morphisms
\mu_{A}
are direct analogues of “multiplication by a matrix”. Section
6.2
is devoted to proving the first equality, and Section
6.1
is devoted to proving the second equality.

In Sections
6.3
and
6.4
we establish several results related to the equality
(\mu_{A})^{*}=\mu_{A^{-1}}
for invertible square matrices
A
(the equality itself is proved in Section
6.4
).

In Section
9
we will deal with a setting very similar to the current one, but lacking the morphism
\varepsilon
and thus the morphism
coev
as well, destroying the self-duality of the object
\mathbf{V}
. For the benefit of that section, we will not use the morphisms
\varepsilon
,
coev
in Sections
6.1
,
6.2
and
6.3
, so that the results in these sections can be applied to the setting of Section
9
as well.

6.1.
Tensor product of morphisms
\mu_{A}

Lemma 6.1.1
.

Let
d,r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0
, and let
A\in Mat_{d\times r_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.
Denote by
\dot{0}
the zero matrix in
Mat_{d\times r_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Then

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&0\end{bmatrix}}=\mu_{A}\otimes(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes r_{2}},\;\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}0&A\end{bmatrix}}=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes r_{2}}\otimes\mu_{A}.

In terms of diagrams, this means:

Proof.

We will prove the first equality; the second one is proved in exactly the same manner.
Clearly, it is enough to prove this for
d=1
: that is, we need to check that

\mu_{[a_{1}\,\ldots\,a_{r_{1}}\,0\,\ldots\,0]}=\mu_{[a_{1}\,\ldots\,a_{r_{1}}]}\otimes(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes r_{2}}

for any
A=[a_{1}\,\ldots\,a_{r_{1}}]\in Mat_{1\times r_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

∎

Proposition 6.1.2
.

Let
A\in Mat_{d_{1}\times r_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
,
B\in Mat_{d_{2}\times r_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be two matrices, and denote:
C=\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}
. Then
\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{B}=\mu_{C}
.

Example 6.1.3
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, this lemma states that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r_{1}},w_{1},\ldots,w_{r_{2}}\in V
, we have:

\mu_{A}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r_{1}})\otimes\mu_{B}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{r_{2}})=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}}(v_{1},\ldots,v_{r_{1}},w_{1},\ldots,w_{r_{2}}).

Indeed, in the setting of Example
5.2.2
, the map
\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{B}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes r_{1}+r_{2}}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes d_{1}+d_{2}}
corresponds to the
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear operator

\displaystyle V^{\times(r_{1}+r_{2})}=V^{\times r_{1}}\dot{\oplus}V^{\times r_{2}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow V^{\times d_{1}}\dot{\oplus}V^{\times d_{2}}=V^{\times(d_{1}+d_{2})}

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{r_{1}}\end{bmatrix}\dot{\oplus}\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
w_{r_{2}}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\longmapsto A\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{r_{1}}\end{bmatrix}\dot{\oplus}B\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
w_{r_{2}}\end{bmatrix}

which is exactly the multiplication of

\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{r_{1}}\\
w_{1}\\
\vdots\\
w_{r_{2}}\end{bmatrix}
by
\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.1.2
.

\mu_{C}
. The morphism
\mu_{C}
is given by the diagram

By Frobenius coalgebra relations (see
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)
), we conclude that this is just
\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{B}
as required.

∎

6.2.
Composing morphisms
\mu_{A}

For the result about composition of morphisms
\mu_{A}
, we will need some auxiliary lemmas.

Lemma 6.2.1
.

Let
k,l\geq 1
. Consider the matrix
\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}\in Mat_{kl\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. We have:

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}=w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

where
(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and
w:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}
is the permutation of tensor factors sending the factor
s+rl
to the position
r+sk
for
0\leq s<l
,
0\leq r<k
. In particular,
\mu_{I_{l}}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.1.1
, we have:

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}0&\ldots&0&1&0&\ldots&0\end{bmatrix}}=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}\otimes(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes r_{2}}
where
r_{1}
is the number of zeroes in the left part of the matrix, and
r_{2}
is the number of zeroes in the right part of the matrix. Using the definition of
\mu_{A}
(see Diagram (
2
)), and the fact that
\varepsilon^{*}
is a counit for
m^{*}
(see Relation
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)
), we obtain the required statement.
∎

This Lemma immediately implies:

Corollary 6.2.2
.

Let
A\in Mat_{s_{1}\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{s_{2}\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Then

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A\\
A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}=\left(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A^{\prime}}\right)\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}.

Lemma 6.2.3
.

Let
k,l\geq 2
. Then the composition
(m^{*})^{it}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}
of
(\dot{+})^{it}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\to\mathbf{V}
and
(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
equals

\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

where
w:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}
is as in Lemma
6.2.1
.

Remark 6.2.4
.

In terms of morphisms
\mu_{A}
, this reads:
\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}1&\ldots&1\\
&\ldots\\
1&\ldots&1\end{bmatrix}}
.

Example 6.2.5
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, the statement of this Lemma translates to the fact that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\in V
, the element

(v_{1}\dot{+}\ldots\dot{+}v_{l})\otimes(v_{1}\dot{+}\ldots\dot{+}v_{l})\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{1}\dot{+}\ldots\dot{+}v_{l})\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}

can be obtained from
v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l}\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes l}
in two ways. The first way is by applying iteratively the map
m^{*}
to
v_{1}\dot{+}\ldots\dot{+}v_{l}\in\mathbf{V}
. The second way is by applying
\dot{+}
to the factors of the tensor

(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})\ldots\otimes(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl}

which in turn is obtained by permutation of factors from the tensor

(v_{1}\otimes v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes(v_{l}\otimes v_{l}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l}).

The latter is just
\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2.3
.

The proof is by double induction on the pair
(k,l)
. For
(k=2,l=2)
, this statement is just the Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
3

)
, stating that
\dot{+}
is a morphism of coalgebras. This relation tells us that

(3)

m^{*}\circ\dot{+}=(\dot{+}\otimes\dot{+})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\sigma\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(m^{*}\otimes m^{*})

(see also
(

⁢
D
R
e

l
3

)
for a diagrammatic version of this equality).

Now, assume the statement holds for
(k=2,l)
for some
l\geq 2
. Let us prove it for
(k=2,l+1)
.
Indeed, in that case by induction assumption

m^{*}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}=\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes 2}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\dot{+}).

Applying (
3
), we see that the right hand side equals
\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes 2}\circ w^{\prime}\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}
. Here
(\dot{+})^{it}
denotes, by abuse of notation, the map
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l+1}\to\mathbf{V}
, and
w^{\prime}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2(l+1)}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2(l+1)}
is a morphism permuting the tensor factors, obtained by precomposing
w
with a permutation
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2(l+1)}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2(l+1)}
swapping the factors
2l,2l+1
. This proves the statement in the case
k=2
.

Next, assume the statement holds for some
(k,l)
,
k,l\geq 2
, and for the pair
(2,l)
. Let us prove it for
k+1,l
. By induction assumption for
(k,l)
and for
(2,l)
, we have:

(m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(m^{*})^{it}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k+1}

equals

\displaystyle(m^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}=\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k+1}\circ(w^{\prime}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ\left((m^{*})^{\otimes l}\otimes\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

where
w^{\prime}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2l}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2l}
is defined analogously to
w
(for
k=2
) and the rightmost
(m^{*})^{it}
stands, by abuse of notation, for the map
\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k+1}
.
Due to the functoriality of
\sigma
and thus of
w
, we may write

\left((m^{*})^{\otimes l}\otimes\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

as a product of a “permutation” morphism
w^{\prime\prime}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k+1)l}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k+1)l}
(permuting the tensor factors) with
\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}
. It is easy to see that the composition
(w^{\prime}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ w^{\prime\prime}
is precisely the permutation
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k+1)l}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k+1)l}
which we needed, and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 6.2.6
.

Let
k,l\geq 2
, and let
A=[a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}]\in Mat_{1\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Then the composition
(m^{*})^{it}\circ\mu_{A}
of
\mu_{A}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\to\mathbf{V}
and
(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
equals

\mu_{A}^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}=\mu_{A}^{\otimes k}\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}

where
w:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}
is as in Lemma
6.2.3
.
