Remark 6.2.7
.

In terms of morphism
\mu_{A}
, this reads:
\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}1\\
\vdots\\
1\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}&\ldots&a_{l}\\
&\ldots\\
a_{1}&\ldots&a_{l}\end{bmatrix}}
.

Example 6.2.8
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, and any matrix
A\in Mat_{1\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, this Lemma states that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{l}\in V
, the element

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{d}\longmapsto\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{d}\dot{A}_{1,j}v_{j}\right)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{d}\dot{A}_{1,j}v_{j}\right)\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}

is obtained by applying

\mu_{A}^{\otimes k}

(see Example
5.2.2
) to the factors of the tensor

(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})\ldots\otimes(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes kl}

which in turn is obtained by permutation of factors from the tensor

v_{1}\otimes v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l}\otimes v_{l}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l}.

The latter is just
\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{l})
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2.6
.

Let us use Lemma
6.2.3
:

\displaystyle(m^{*})^{it}\circ\mu_{A}=(m^{*})^{it}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}=\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}

By Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
, stating that
\mu_{a}
is a morphism of coalgebras, we have:

\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}.

Furthermore, since the symmetry morphism
\sigma:\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}
is functorial, so is
w
, hence

w\circ\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}\right)^{\otimes k}=\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w

and we conclude that

\displaystyle(m^{*})^{it}\circ\mu_{A}

\displaystyle=\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}=\left((\dot{+})^{it}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{l}\mu_{a_{i}}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

\displaystyle=\mu_{A}^{\otimes k}\circ w\circ\left((m^{*})^{it}\right)^{\otimes l}

as required.
∎

From Lemma
6.2.6
we conclude:

Lemma 6.2.9
.

For any
A\in Mat_{k\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, let
\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}\\
\vdots\\
I_{k}\end{bmatrix}\in Mat_{rk\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Then we have:

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}\\
\vdots\\
I_{k}\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A\\
\vdots\\
A\end{bmatrix}}=(\mu_{A})^{\otimes r}\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}

where the rightmost matrix has
rl
rows.

Proof.

The second equality follows from Corollary
6.2.2
.

For the first equality, we argue as follows. Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
denote the rows of
A
. By Lemma
6.2.1
and Corollary
6.2.2
, we have:

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}\\
\vdots\\
I_{k}\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{A}=w_{1}\circ\left((m^{*})^{it_{1}}\right)^{\otimes k}\circ\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{A_{i}}\right)\circ w_{2}\circ\left((m^{*})^{it_{2}}\right)^{\otimes l}

where
(m^{*})^{it_{1}}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}
,
(m^{*})^{it_{2}}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and
w_{1}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes rk}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes rk}
,
w_{2}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}
as in Lemma
6.2.1
.
Now, Lemma
6.2.6
applied to the right hand side gives the desired result.
∎

We are now ready to show that the operators
\mu_{A}
behave as “multiplication by matrix
A
”:

Proposition 6.2.10
.

For any
B\in Mat_{r\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A\in Mat_{k\times l}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, we have:

\mu_{B}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{BA}.

Proof.

First, assume that
r=k=1
(so
B
is just a scalar,
b
, and
A
is a row matrix). By the Relations
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)
from Definition
5.1.1
describing the “
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-module structure on
\mathbf{V}
”, we have:

\mu_{b}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{b}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}\circ(\mu_{a_{11}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mu_{a_{1l}})=(\dot{+})^{it}\circ((\mu_{b}\circ\mu_{a_{11}})\otimes\ldots\otimes(\mu_{b}\circ\mu_{a_{1l}}))=(\dot{+})^{it}\circ(\mu_{ba_{11}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mu_{ba_{1l}})

which clearly equals
\mu_{bA}
.

Next, let
k,l
be any, and consider the composition of

(\dot{+})^{it}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}
with
\mu_{A}
. Since the composition
(\dot{+})^{it}\circ((\dot{+})^{it}\otimes\ldots\otimes(\dot{+})^{it}):\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}
is just
(\dot{+})^{it}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes kl}\to\mathbf{V}
, we conclude that

(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{[1,\ldots,1]A}
.

Now, assume that
r=1
and
k,l
are arbitrary. Then
B=[b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}]
is a row matrix, and

\mu_{B}\circ\mu_{A}=(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{b_{i}}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{A_{i}}\circ(m^{*})^{it}=(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\bigotimes_{i=1}^{k}\mu_{b_{i}A_{i}}\circ(m^{*})^{it}

where
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
are the rows of
A
:

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

The computation above for
\mu_{b_{i}}\circ\mu_{A}
then implies that
\mu_{B}\circ\mu_{A}=\mu_{BA}
in this case.

Now we tackle the most general case.

We write
B_{1},\ldots,B_{r}
for the rows of
B
. Then by Corollary
6.2.2
and Lemma
6.2.9
we have:

\displaystyle\mu_{B}\circ\mu_{A}

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{B_{i}}\right)\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}\\
\vdots\\
I_{k}\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{A}=\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{B_{i}}\right)\circ(\mu_{A})^{\otimes r}\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}=\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{r}\mu_{B_{i}\,A}\right)\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{l}\\
\vdots\\
I_{l}\end{bmatrix}}

Here the last equality follows from the case
r=1
. The right hand side is now
\mu_{BA}
by Corollary
6.2.2
.
Diagrammatically, this means:

This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Finally, the following two results will be useful for Section
9
:

Corollary 6.2.11
.

Let
A\in Mat_{d\times r_{1}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B\in Mat_{d\times r_{2}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. Then we have:

\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&B\end{bmatrix}}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{d}&I_{d}\end{bmatrix}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{B})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
6.2.10
,

\begin{bmatrix}A&B\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{d}&I_{d}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}\;\Longrightarrow\;\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&B\end{bmatrix}}=\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{d}&I_{d}\end{bmatrix}}\circ\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}}.

Proposition
6.1.2
now implies the required statement.
∎

6.3.
Morphisms
\mu_{A}
for invertible matrices

The main goal of this section is to show that for
A\in GL_{d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
,
(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\mu_{A}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}
(this is proved in Corollary
6.3.8
); this statement is the analogue of the linear algebra statement “The only solution to the system of linear equations
Ax=0
for
A\in GL_{d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
is the solution
x=0
”.

Along the way, we prove several related statements (consequences of the Cancellation Axiom
(

⁢
R
e

l
4

)
) which will be very useful to us later on.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

We have the following equality of morphisms
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 3}\to{\mathbbm{1}}
:

\hbox to94.3pt{\vbox to47.35pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 15.84909pt\lower-31.4982pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

Example 6.3.2
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, this statement translates to

\forall v,u,w\in V,\;\;\delta_{v\dot{+}u,w\dot{+}u}=\delta_{v,w}.

Proof.

Let us compute the left hand side of the above equation, using the definition
ev:=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m
:

Lemma 6.3.3
.

We have the following equality of morphisms
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 3}\to{\mathbbm{1}}
:

ev\circ(\dot{+}\otimes\operatorname{Id})=ev\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\dot{+})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\mu_{-1}\otimes\operatorname{Id}).

Example 6.3.4
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, this statement translates to

\forall v,u,w\in V,\;\;\delta_{v\dot{+}u,w}=\delta_{v,w\dot{-}u}.

Lemma 6.3.5
.

For any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
, we have
ev\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})=ev
.

Proof.

Using Relations
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
which state that
\mu_{a}
commutes with both counit and multiplication (that is,
\varepsilon^{*}\circ\mu_{a}=\varepsilon^{*}
and
m\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})=\mu_{a}\circ m
), we obtain that

ev\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})=\varepsilon^{*}\circ\mu_{a}\circ m=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m=ev.

∎

Lemma 6.3.6
.

For any
A\in GL_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the map

\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A}):\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to{\mathbbm{1}}

is equal to
\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}
.

Example 6.3.7
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
k=2
and

A=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Then
\mu_{A}(v_{1}\otimes v_{2})=(v_{1}\dot{+}v_{2})\otimes v_{2}
for any
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
. Recall that
\{v_{1}\otimes v_{2}|\,v_{1},v_{2}\in V\}
forms a basis in
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes 2}
, so the map
\mu_{A}^{*}
takes
v^{\prime}_{1}\otimes v^{\prime}_{2}
to
(v^{\prime}_{1}\dot{-}v^{\prime}_{2})\otimes v^{\prime}_{2}
for any
v^{\prime}_{1},v^{\prime}_{2}\in V
. Hence
\mu_{A}^{*}=\mu_{A^{-1}}
where
A=\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. This implies the statement of the lemma in the setting of our example.

Proof of Lemma
6.3.6
.

Let us write
A
as a product of elementary matrices (these are matrices obtained from the identity matrix by one single elementary row operation). Recall that for this it is enough to use the following elementary matrices:

•

E_{R_{i}\longleftrightarrow R_{i+1}}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, the permutation matrix for a simple reflection.

•

E_{R_{i}\longleftarrow aR_{i}}
for
a\neq 0
and for
1\leq i\leq k
. This is the diagonal matrix with
a
in position
(i,i)
and
1
’s on the rest of the diagonal.

•

E_{R_{i}\longleftarrow R_{i}+R_{i+1}}
for
1\leq i<k
. This matrix differs from the matrix
\operatorname{Id}_{k\times k}
by a single entry
(i,i+1)
, which is
1
.

Due to Proposition
6.2.10
, it is enough to prove the equality
\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A})=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}
for the elementary matrices
A
as above.
We now do this case-by-case:

Let
A=E_{R_{i}\longleftrightarrow R_{i+1}}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
In that case, the statement boils down to proving that

\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}\circ(\sigma\otimes\sigma)=ev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}
, where
\sigma:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}
is the symmetry morphism. But this is true for any special commutative Frobenius algebra in a rigid symmetric monoidal category, so there is nothing to check.

Let
A=E_{R_{i}\longleftarrow aR_{i}}
for
a\neq 0
and
1\leq i\leq k
.
In that case, the statement boils down to proving that

ev\circ(\mu_{a}\otimes\mu_{a})=ev
, which is proved in Lemma
6.3.5
.

Let
A=E_{R_{i}\longleftarrow R_{i}+R_{i+1}}
for
1\leq i<k
.
In that case, the statement boils down to proving that
\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A})=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}
for

A=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
. Equivalently, we need to prove that

ev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A^{t}})=ev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}}
.
Diagramatically,
\mu_{A}
looks like this:

which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 6.3.8
.

For any
A\in GL_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, we have:
(z^{*})^{\otimes k}\circ\mu_{A}=(z^{*})^{\otimes k}
.

Example 6.3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
. This corollary states that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
and any
A\in GL_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, the element

\mu_{A}\left(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\right)=\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{1,j}v_{j}\right)\otimes\ldots\otimes\left(\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{k,j}v_{j}\right)\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}

equals
\dot{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes\dot{0}
iff
v_{1}=\ldots=v_{k}=\dot{0}
. In this setting, it is easy to see that this statement is true: indeed,
\mu_{A}\left(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}\right)=\dot{0}\otimes\ldots\otimes\dot{0}
iff the system of equalities

\begin{cases}\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{1,j}v_{j}=\dot{0}\\
\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{2,j}v_{j}=\dot{0}\\
\ldots\\
\dot{\sum}_{j=1}^{k}\dot{A}_{k,j}v_{j}=\dot{0}\end{cases}

holds, which, as we know from linear algebra, happens exactly when
v_{1}=\ldots=v_{k}=\dot{0}
(due to the fact that
A
is invertible).

Proof.

Recall that
z^{*}=ev\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z):\mathbf{V}\to{\mathbbm{1}}
. Thus

(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{A}=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes z^{\otimes k})=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes(\mu_{A}\circ z^{\otimes k}))

The second equality follows from the fact that
z^{\otimes k}=\mu_{A}\circ z^{\otimes k}
, which is a direct consequence of Relations (
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)
)). By Lemma
6.3.6
, we have:

\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes(\mu_{A}\circ z^{\otimes k}))=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z^{\otimes k})=\overline{ev}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z^{\otimes k})=(z^{*})^{\otimes k}.

∎

The following lemma will be useful in Section
9.4
.

Lemma 6.3.10
.

(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes z^{*})\circ m^{*}=m\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes z)=z\circ z^{*}.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Thus we showed that

m\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes z)=\left(coev\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes z)\right)\otimes z.

Finally, we use the functoriality of tensor product to establish that

\left(coev\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes z)\right)\otimes z=z^{*}\otimes z=z\circ z^{*},

and we are done.
∎

6.4.
Duality

Lemma 6.4.1
.

The morphisms
ev:=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m
,
coev:=m^{*}\circ\varepsilon
make
\mathbf{V}
a self-dual object in
\mathcal{C}
. The categorical dimension of this object is
\dim(\mathbf{V})=\varepsilon^{*}\circ\varepsilon
.

Example 6.4.2
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, we have

(\varepsilon^{*}\circ\varepsilon)(1)=\sum_{v\in V}\varepsilon^{*}(v)=\sum_{v\in V}1=q^{n}=\dim\mathbf{V}_{n}.

Proof.

The first statement is a standard fact for Frobenius algebras. We only need to check the “snake relations”

(ev\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes coev)=\operatorname{Id}=(\operatorname{Id}\otimes ev)\circ(coev\otimes\operatorname{Id})

and they follow directly from the axioms of a Frobenius algebra along with the Frobenius relations (see
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)(

⁢
F

r
1

)
). For the second statement, recall that

\dim(\mathbf{V})=ev\circ\sigma\circ coev=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m\circ\sigma\circ coev=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m\circ coev=\varepsilon^{*}\circ m\circ m^{*}\circ\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{*}\circ\varepsilon.

Here the second equality is due to the commutativity of
m
, while the last equality is due to the Speciality Relation (see
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)(

⁢
F

r
2

)
).
∎

Remark 6.4.3
.

The self-duality
\mathbf{V}\cong\mathbf{V}^{*}
makes the morphisms
m
,
m^{*}
dual to each other, and the morphisms
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{*}
dual to each other.

Lemma 6.4.4
.

The morphism
(\mu_{A})^{*}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
is
\mu_{A^{-1}}
.

Proof.

This is a direct corollary of Lemma
6.3.6
.
∎

Corollary 6.4.5
.

We have
\mu_{a}\circ\varepsilon=\varepsilon
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
.

Proof.

This follows directly from Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
, which states that
\varepsilon^{*}\circ\mu_{a}=\varepsilon^{*}
, and Lemma
6.3.5
.
∎

Example 6.4.6
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, the statement of Lemma
6.3.5
translates to

\delta_{\dot{a}v,\dot{a}w}=\delta_{v,w}
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
,
v,w\in V
, and Corollary
6.4.5
translates to
\dot{a}\sum_{v\in V}v=\sum_{v\in V}v
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
.

7.
Morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear rigid SM category and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
.

In this section we define morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}
,
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
in
\mathcal{C}
which will serve as analogues of the maps
f_{R}
defined in Sections
3
,
4
. We will then prove that they satisfy the same relations on their compositions and tensor products as their counterparts in Section
4
; this will allow us to construct a SM functor from the Deligne category into
\mathcal{C}
in Section
8
.

The following definition is the first step toward defining morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}
.

Definition 7.0.1
.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspace.
Let
d=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}R
. Let
B\in Mat_{d\times r}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be a matrix of rank
d
such that
Row(B)=R
.
Consider the morphism

\displaystyle\phi_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}\to{\mathbbm{1}}

\displaystyle\phi_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes r}\xrightarrow{\mu_{B}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes d}\xrightarrow{(z^{*})^{\otimes d}}{\mathbbm{1}}

Diagrammatically, we draw
\phi_{R}
as follows:

B
\circ
\circ
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

First of all, we check that
\phi_{R}
is well-defined:

Lemma 7.0.2
.

The morphism
\phi_{R}
does not depend on the choice of the basis in
R
.

Proof.

Let
B^{\prime}\in Mat_{d\times r}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be any matrix whose rows are a basis of
R
. Then
B^{\prime}=A\circ B
for some
A\in GL_{d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, so

(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{B^{\prime}}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{A}\circ\mu_{B^{\prime}}.

By Corollary
6.3.8
, we have:
(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{A}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}
and so
(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{B^{\prime}}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{B}
, as required.
∎

Example 7.0.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, and
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspace. For any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\in V
, the morphism
\phi_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes r}\to{\mathbb{C}}
takes
v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{r}
to
1
iff
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{r})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}}}}
(notation as in Section
3
) and to
0
otherwise.

It turns out that the tensor product of
\phi_{R}
’s is again a morphism of the same form. The following is a direct consequence of Proposition
6.1.2
:

Corollary 7.0.4
.

Let
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}}
and
R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}}
be
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces.
Let
d_{i}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}R_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Let
B^{(i)}\in Mat_{d_{i}\times r_{i}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be a matrix whose rows form a basis of
R_{i}

for
i=1,2
. Then
\phi_{R_{1}}\otimes\phi_{R_{2}}=\phi_{S}
where
S=R_{1}\times R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
; a basis for
S
is given by rows of the block matrix
C=\begin{bmatrix}B^{(1)}&0\\
0&B^{(2)}\end{bmatrix}
.

Example 7.0.5
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
V:={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
, this corollary states that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{r_{1}},w_{1},\ldots,w_{r_{2}}\in V
, we have:
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{r_{1}}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{r_{2}})\in(R_{1}\times R_{2})^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
iff both
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{r_{1}})\in(R_{1})^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
and
(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{r_{2}})\in(R_{2})^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}
.

Definition 7.0.6
.

Let
s,k\geq 0
. Consider the isomorphism

\displaystyle T:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k},{\mathbbm{1}})

\displaystyle T(f):\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{f\otimes\operatorname{Id}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\xrightarrow{ev_{V^{\otimes k}}}{\mathbbm{1}},

Let
s,k,l\geq 0
. Consider the operation
\circledast
defined by

\displaystyle\circledast:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k},{\mathbbm{1}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l},{\mathbbm{1}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l},{\mathbbm{1}})

\displaystyle\phi\circledast\psi:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Id}\otimes coev_{V^{\otimes k}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}}\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\xrightarrow{\phi\otimes\psi}{\mathbbm{1}}.

The following lemma is proved in
[
D2
, Lemma 8.5(i)]
:

Lemma 7.0.7
.

Let
f:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and
g:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
. Then
T(g\circ f)=T(f)\circledast T(g)
.

Further properties of the isomorphism
T
can be found in
[
D2
, Lemma 8.5]
. We also have the following straightforward statement:

Lemma 7.0.8
.

Let
f:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s_{1}}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k_{1}}
and
g:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s_{2}}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k_{2}}
. Then
T(g\otimes f)=T(g)\otimes T(f)
.

Definition 7.0.9
.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
. We define
\widetilde{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
as
\widetilde{f}_{R}:=T^{-1}(\phi_{R})
.

Example 7.0.10
.

(1)

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
, the morphism
\widetilde{f}_{R}:=T^{-1}(\phi_{R})
is just
f_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
as defined in Section
3
:

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\longmapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in V^{\times k}\\
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\end{subarray}}w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}

for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in V
.

(2)

Similarly, for
\mathcal{C}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
and
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{t}=[1]
, the morphism
\widetilde{f}_{R}:=T^{-1}(\phi_{R})
is just
f_{R}:[s]\to[k]
as defined in Section
4
.

As can be expected from this example, we have, in general:

Lemma 7.0.11
.

Let
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+s_{1}}
and
R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}+s_{2}}
be
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces.
Let
d_{i}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}R_{i}
for
i=1,2
. Let
B^{(i)}\in Mat_{d_{i}\times r_{i}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be a matrix whose rows form a basis of
R_{i}

for
i=1,2
. Then
\widetilde{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{f}_{R_{2}}=\widetilde{f}_{S}
where
S=R_{1}\times R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
; a basis for
S
is given by the block matrix
C=\begin{bmatrix}B^{(1)}&0\\
0&B^{(2)}\end{bmatrix}
.

Proof.

Since
T
is an isomorphism, it is enough to check that

T\left(\widetilde{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{f}_{R_{2}}\right)=T\left(\widetilde{f}_{S}\right)

But by Lemma
7.0.8
, we have:

T\left(\widetilde{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{f}_{R_{2}}\right)=T\left(\widetilde{f}_{R_{1}}\right)\otimes T\left(\widetilde{f}_{R_{2}}\right)=\phi_{R_{1}}\otimes\phi_{R_{2}}=\phi_{S}=T\left(\widetilde{f}_{S}\right)

(the equality
\phi_{R_{1}}\otimes\phi_{R_{2}}=\phi_{S}
was proved in Corollary
7.0.4
).

∎

The following proposition generalizes Proposition
3.2.2
:

Proposition 7.0.12
.

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
and
S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+l}
be
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces. Then

\widetilde{f}_{S}\circ\widetilde{f}_{R}=(\dim\mathbf{V})^{d(R,S)}\widetilde{f}_{S\star R}

where
d(R,S)
and
S\star R
are defined in Definition
3.2.1
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.0.7
, it is enough to prove that

(4)

\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}=(\dim\mathbf{V})^{d(R,S)}\phi_{S\star R}.

Let
B\in Mat_{d_{1}\times(s+k)}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be a matrix whose rows form a basis of
R
and let

C\in Mat_{d_{2}\times(k+l)}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be a matrix whose rows form a basis of
S
.

We prove the equality (
4
) in several steps, which we outline below. After that, we will provide the missing details for each step.

Step 1:

We show that

\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}=(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})

for the matrix
D\in Mat_{(d_{1}+d_{2})\times(s+k+l)}
defined as

D={\left[\begin{array}[]{cccccc}&&B&|&0&\\
\hline\cr&0&|&C&&\end{array}\right]}

(note that this is not a block matrix!). Then

Row(D)=R\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S=(R,0)+(0,S)\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k+l},

though the rows of
D
do not have to be linearly independent.

Step 2:

We show that
(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D}=(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D^{\prime}}
for any
D^{\prime}\in Mat_{(d_{1}+d_{2})\times(s+k+l)}
which is row-equivalent to
D
(that is,
Row(D)=Row(D^{\prime})
).

Step 3:

In particular, the equality in Step 2 is true for a matrix
D^{\prime}
obtained from
D
as follows: using elementary row operations, we can bring
D
to a matrix of the form

D^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}&G&|&M&|&H&\\
\hline\cr&E&|&0&|&F&\\
\hline\cr&0&|&0&|&0&\end{bmatrix}

Here the middle
k
columns form a matrix in row echelon form, which we denote by
\begin{bmatrix}M\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}

where
M
has full row rank.
The middle part
\begin{bmatrix}&E&|&0&|&F&\end{bmatrix}
has linearly independent rows whose middle
k
coordinates are zero (we denote the number of such rows by
r
). The bottom part consists of zero rows.

We denote the
r\times(s+l)
matrix
\begin{bmatrix}&E&|&F&\end{bmatrix}
by
B\star C
.

Here is how these new matrices relate to the original statement: the rows of
B\star C
are clearly linearly independent and span the subspace
R\star S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+l}
. The rank of
M
is the dimension of the projection of
R\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S
on the subspace
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k+l}
, so
k-rk(M)=d(R,S)
.

Step 4:

We show that

(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})=(\dim\mathbf{V})^{d(R,S)}(z^{*})^{\otimes r}\circ\mu_{B\star C}.

The left hand side has been shown to be equal to
\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}
, while the right hand side is
(\dim\mathbf{V})^{d(R,S)}\phi_{R\star S}
, which proves the required statement.

We now provide the missing details in each step:

Step 1:

Recall that
\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}=(\phi_{R}\otimes\phi_{S})\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes coev_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}}\right)
. Diagrammatically,
\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}
can be drawn as

{B_{1}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{B_{2}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{B_{s+k}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{1}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{2}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{k+l}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

where we have
s
string bottom endpoints on the left,
l
string bottom endpoints on the right, and
k
“cups” in between.
This diagram can be drawn alternatively as

{B_{1}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{B_{2}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{B_{s+k}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{1}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{2}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{C_{k+l}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\bullet

Here in the bottom we have
s
strands on the left,
l
strands on the right, and
k
“tails” of the form
\bullet
in the middle (these stand for
\varepsilon^{\otimes k}
). This shows that the equality

\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}=(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})

holds.

Step 2:
We need to show that
(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{X}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{X^{\prime}}
for any matrices
X
,
X^{\prime}
of same size (with
d
rows) which are row-equivalent. Indeed, for any two such matrices there exists
A\in GL_{d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
such that
X^{\prime}=AX
, so by Proposition
6.2.10
, we have:
\mu_{X^{\prime}}=\mu_{A}\circ\mu_{X}
. Thus

\displaystyle(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{X^{\prime}}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{A}\circ\mu_{X}=(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\circ\mu_{X}

where the last equality is due to Corollary
6.3.8
.

Step 3:
In this step there is nothing to prove.

Step 4:

Let us denote by
D^{\prime}_{1},D^{\prime}_{2},\ldots,D^{\prime}_{d_{1}+d_{2}}
the rows of the matrix
D^{\prime}
, and let

\widetilde{M}:=\begin{bmatrix}&G&|&M&|&H&\end{bmatrix}.

We will denote by
\widetilde{r}
the number of rows in
\widetilde{M}
(so
\widetilde{r}=rk(\widetilde{M})=rk(M)=k-d(R,S)
).

Consider the morphism
(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})
. Diagrammatically, we can draw it as follows:

(5)

s
strands on the left,
l
strands on the right, and
k
“tails” of the form
\bullet
in the middle (these stand for
\varepsilon^{\otimes k}
).

Let us look at each of the rows
D^{\prime}_{i}
separately. For any row
D^{\prime}_{i}
having the form
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{s},0,\ldots,0,a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots a^{\prime}_{l}]
, set

D^{\prime\prime}_{i}:=[a_{1},\ldots,a_{s},a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots a^{\prime}_{l}].

By the properties of the zero map
z
, we have:
\dot{+}\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z)=\dot{+}\circ(z\otimes\operatorname{Id})=\operatorname{Id}
(
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
2

)
). So we obtain:

\mu_{D^{\prime}_{i}}=\mu_{D^{\prime\prime}_{i}}\,\circ\,\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}}\right).

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

On the right hand side of this equation, in the bottom we have
s
strands on the left,
l
strands on the right, and
k
“tails” of the form
\bullet
in the middle (these stand for
(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes k}
).

Now, recall that
\varepsilon^{*}
is a counit of
m^{*}
(
(

⁢
D
F
r
o

b
1

)
):

(\varepsilon^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id})\circ m^{*}=(\operatorname{Id}\otimes\varepsilon^{*})\circ m^{*}=\operatorname{Id}.

So for any
p\geq 2
, the composition of

(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes p}
and
\operatorname{Id}\otimes\varepsilon^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Id}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes p}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(p-1)}
gives
(m^{*})^{it}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes p-1}
(no matter on which factor
\varepsilon^{*}
acts).

In other words, we can just erase the box corresponding to a zeroes row, together with all the strings leading into in, and obtain an equivalent diagram.

) is equivalent to the following diagram:

The number of the bottom strands and “tails” is as in (
5
). Now let us consider the matrix
M
. Since the matrix
M
is in row canonical form, there is an entry
1
in each row of
M
so that the remaining entries in this column of
M
are zeroes.
For each
i
, we denote by
j_{i}
the smallest index of the column such that
M_{i,j_{i}}=1
,
M_{i,j^{\prime}}=0
for all
j^{\prime}<j_{i}
.

Then the above argument shows that the
j_{i}
-th factor
\varepsilon^{*}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}
(drawn as the
j_{i}
-th fork with a “tail” of the form
\bullet
) only needs to be connected to the rectangle representing
\mu_{\widetilde{M}_{i}}
.

This implies that we can redraw the above diagram as

{\widetilde{M}_{1}}

l

k-\widetilde{r}=d(R,S)

\bullet
in the middle. The row canonical form of
M
ensures that for each
i=1,\ldots,\widetilde{r}
we have:

In the bottom of this diagram we have
s+l
strands on the left, and
k-\widetilde{r}=d(R,S)
strings of the form
\bullet
\bullet
on the right. The diagram

{B\star C}
\circ

\cdot
\cdot
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

is the diagram of
\phi_{R\star S}
by Step 3. Thus we proved that

(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})=\phi_{R\star S}\otimes(\varepsilon^{*}\circ\varepsilon)^{\otimes d(R,S)}.

By Lemma
6.4.1
,
\varepsilon^{*}\circ\varepsilon=(\dim\mathbf{V})\operatorname{Id}_{{\mathbbm{1}}}
, and we conclude that

\phi_{R}\circledast\phi_{S}=(z^{*})^{\otimes(d_{1}+d_{2})}\circ\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s}}\otimes\varepsilon^{\otimes k}\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}})=(\dim\mathbf{V})^{d(R,S)}\phi_{R\star S}.

This proves the required statement.

∎

3.2.2
.

.

Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and assume that
a_{i}\neq 0
for some

Example 7.0.14
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
as in the lemma. The lemma states that for any
v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1},v_{i+1},\ldots,v_{k}\in V
, the equality

\dot{\sum}_{1\leq j\leq k,j\neq i}\dot{a}_{j}v_{j}\,\dot{+}\,\dot{a}_{i}v=\dot{0}

holds for exactly one
v\in V
, so the vector
\dot{0}
appears in the sum

\sum_{v\in V}\,\left(\dot{\sum}_{1\leq j\leq k,j\neq i}\dot{a}_{j}v_{j}\,\dot{+}\,\dot{a}_{i}v\right)

with coefficient
1
, which is precisely
(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes(k-1)}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k})
.

Proof.

First of all, recall that Corollary
6.4.5
states:
a_{i}\neq 0
implies
\mu_{a_{i}}\circ\varepsilon=\varepsilon=\mu_{1}\circ\varepsilon
. Hence we may assume that
a_{i}=1
from now on.

Next, it is enough to check the statement for
a_{1}=\ldots=a_{k}=1
. Indeed, if we show that

(7)

z^{*}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k-i)}}\right)=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes(k-1)}

then we will have

\displaystyle z^{*}\circ\mu_{[a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}]}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k-i)}}\right)=z^{*}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{a_{j}}\right)\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k-i)}}\right)

\displaystyle=z^{*}\circ(\dot{+})^{it}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}}\otimes\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes(k-i)}}\right)\circ\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{a_{j}}\right)=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes(k-1)}\circ\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{a_{j}}\right)=(\varepsilon^{*})^{\otimes(k-1)}

The equality between the first and the last lines follows from the assumption
a_{i}=1
, while the last equality follows from Relation
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
stating that
\varepsilon^{*}\circ\mu_{a}=\varepsilon^{*}
for any
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
(see also
(

⁢
D
R
e

l
1

)
).

So it is enough to prove Equation (
7
), i.e. that

(8)

\hbox to102.83pt{\vbox to44.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 42.87914pt\lower-20.49316pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -59.06 0 L -11.81 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 L 82.68 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}

,

2

8.
Universal properties

This section contains several of the main theorems. We prove the universal property of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
and of
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
and identify our category with the category studied by Knop in
[
K
,
K2
]
.

8.1.
Universal property of the Deligne category

Theorem 8.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Karoubi additive rigid SM category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
. Let
t=\dim(\mathbf{V})
. Then there exists a SM functor

F_{\mathbf{V}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C},\;\;\mathbf{V}_{t}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

Proof.

We may assume
\mathcal{C}
to be a strict rigid symmetric monoidal category (see
[
ML
]
).

Define a functor
F:\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})\to\mathcal{C}
by sending
[k]
to
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and for each linear subspace
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+l}
,
F
sends
f_{R}:[k]\to[l]
(as defined in Section
4
) to
\widetilde{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
(as defined in Definition
7.0.9
). We then extend
F
by linearity to the entire
\operatorname{Hom}([k],[l])
.

We claim that this defines a functor: indeed, by Proposition
7.0.12
we have: for any linear subspaces
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k},S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+l}
,
F(f_{S}\circ f_{R})=\widetilde{f}_{S}\circ\widetilde{f}_{R}
as required.

Now, to check that this is a monoidal functor, we only need to check that
F(f_{R_{1}}\otimes f_{R_{2}})=\widetilde{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{f}_{R_{2}}
for any linear subspaces
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+s_{1}},R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}+s_{2}}
. This is proved in Lemma
7.0.11
. By the construction of the Karoubi envelope, the functor
F
extends uniquely to a functor
F_{X}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C}
.

The fact that the functor is symmetric follows from the fact that the symmetry morphism

\sigma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k},\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l})
is given by
\widetilde{f}_{R^{\prime}}
where

R^{\prime}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},-b_{1},\ldots,-b_{k},-a_{1},\ldots,-a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k+2l}.

∎

Example 8.1.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\mathbf{V}=\mathbf{V}_{n}
. The functor

F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\;\;\mathbf{V}_{t=q^{n}}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}_{n}

is the semisimplification of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
seen in Proposition
4.0.1
.

Corollary 8.1.3
.

The functor

Fun^{\otimes}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}},\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C},t),\;\;F\longmapsto F(\mathbf{V}_{t})

is an equivalence of categories. Here

•

Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C},t)
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of
t
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius spaces and isomorphisms between them.

•

Fun^{\otimes}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}},\mathcal{C}\right)
is the category of SM functors
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C}
and natural SM isomorphisms.

Proof.

To define the inverse functor

Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C},t)\longrightarrow Fun^{\otimes}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}},\mathcal{C}\right),\;\;\mathbf{V}\longmapsto F_{\mathbf{V}}

we just need to explain how an isomorphism
\phi:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
extends to a natural SM isomorphism
F_{\mathbf{V}}\to F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}
. Indeed, by the construction of the functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}
,
\phi
defines isomorphisms
F_{\mathbf{V}}([s])\longrightarrow F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}([s])
for each
s\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, and these isomorphisms commute with the maps
\widetilde{f}_{R}
constructed in the proof of Theorem
8.1.1
. Thus
\phi
induces a natural SM isomorphism between the restricted functors
F_{\mathbf{V}},F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})\rightrightarrows\mathcal{C}
. By the properties of additive Karoubi envelopes, this isomorphism extends uniquely to a natural SM isomorphism
F_{\mathbf{V}}\to F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Corollary 8.1.4
.

Any morphism
[s]\to[k]
in
\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
(respectively, in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
) can be presented as
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear combination of compositions
and
\otimes
products
of morphisms
m,m^{*},\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{*}
,
\sigma
(the symmetry morphism),
z
,
\dot{+}
and
\mu_{a}
for
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
.

Proof.

For
\mathcal{C}:=\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t})
, Theorem
8.1.1
showed that any morphism
f_{R}
is a composition of the morphisms above.

Since in this case
f_{R}
’s form a basis for
Hom([s],[k])
(by construction of the Deligne category), the required statement now follows.
∎

8.2.
Relation to the category defined in
[
K
,
K2
]

In this section we briefly recall the definition of the category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
as given in
[
K
,
K2
]
and explain why it is equivalent to our definition.
We will use the notation as in
Section

3.3
(see
3.3.2
): given
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k},S\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+s}
, we denote by
S\diamond R
the image of the map
R\times_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}}S\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+s}
and by
e(S,R)
the dimension of the kernel of this map.

Define
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
as a category whose objects are non-negative integers; the objects will be denoted by
[k]
,
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
.
We set

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}([s],[k])={\mathbb{C}}Rel_{s,k}

where the morphism
[s]\to[k]
corresponding to
R\in Rel_{s,k}
is denoted by
h_{R}
. The composition of morphisms given as follows: for
R\in Rel_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel_{k,l}
we set

h_{S}\circ h_{R}:=t^{e(S,R)}h_{S\diamond R}.

The tensor structure of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is given by
[l]\otimes[k]:=[l+k]
, and tensor products of morphisms are given as follows: for
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspaces
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}}
and
R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}}
, set
h_{R_{1}}\otimes h_{R_{2}}:=h_{R_{1}\times R_{2}}
where
R_{1}\times R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}
is considered as an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear subspace.

Each object is then self-dual via the evaluation and coevaluation maps

h_{R}:[k]\otimes[k]\to{\mathbbm{1}},\;\;h_{R}:{\mathbbm{1}}\to[k]\otimes[k]

where
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k}
is given by
R=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},a_{k},\ldots,a_{1})|a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}.

The symmetric structure is given by the morphisms

\sigma=h_{R}:[l]\otimes[k]\to[k]\otimes[l]
where

R=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k+2l}.

Let
\mathcal{K}
be the Karoubi additive envelope of
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
. This is the Deligne category corresponding to finite linear groups as defined in
[
K
,
K2
]
.

Lemma 8.2.1
.

We have an equivalence of symmetric monoidal categories:

\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime},[k]\mapsto[k],f_{R}\mapsto h_{R^{\perp}},

which induces an equivalence of symmetric monoidal categories

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\cong\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

Lemma
3.3.3
implies that the correspondence
[k]\mapsto[k],f_{R}\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{h}}_{R^{\perp}}
defines a functor, and the fact that it is monoidal means that
R_{1}^{\perp}\times R_{2}^{\perp}=(R_{1}\times R_{2})^{\perp}
for any
R_{1}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{1}}
and
R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{r_{2}}
(the latter statement being straightforward).
To show that this is a symmetric functor, we need to check that symmetry morphisms correspond to symmetry morphisms: that is, to check that for

R=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},-b_{1},\ldots,-b_{k},-a_{1},\ldots,-a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k+2l}

we have:

R^{\perp}=\{(a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{l},b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{k},b^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,b^{\prime}_{k},a^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,a^{\prime}_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a^{\prime}_{i},b^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}.

Checking this equality is again straightforward; so we proved that our correspondence defines a symmetric monoidal functor. Clearly, this functor is an equivalence of categories
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, with an inverse functor given by

\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime},[k]\mapsto[k],h_{R}\mapsto f_{R^{\perp}}.

Finally, the functor
\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
induces an additive symmetric monoidal functor

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{K}
, and the fact that the former is an equivalence implies that so is the latter.
∎

The equivalence above now allows us to use the results by from
[
K
,
K2
]
concerning the semisimplicity of
\mathcal{K}
:

Theorem 8.2.2

(F. Knop)
.

The category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
is abelian semisimple for
t\notin\{q^{n}|n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\}
. For
t=q^{n}
, the category
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
is not abelian nor semisimple, and its semisimplification is
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
.

8.3.
Universal property of the category
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))

We begin with a reminder on the universal property of the category
\mathrm{Rep}(S_{n})
, where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group on
n
letters. This property follows e.g. from
[
D2
]

[
CO2
]
, but we prove it here for completeness of exposition.

Lemma 8.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category, and let
A
be a commutative Frobenius object in
\mathcal{C}
such that
\wedge^{N}A=0
for some
N\geq 1
. Then
\dim A=n
for some integer
n
, and there exists a SM functor

\mathrm{Rep}(S_{n})\to\mathcal{C},

sending the
n
-dimensional permutation representation of
S_{n}
to
A
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{A}
be the full tensor subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
generated by
A
(it is the full subcategory whose objects are subquotients of finite direct sums of tensor powers of
A
). It is enough to establish that
\dim_{\mathcal{C}}A=n
for some
n
, and the existence of a SM functor

\widetilde{F}_{A}:\mathrm{Rep}(S_{n})\to\mathcal{C}_{A},\;\;A_{n}\longmapsto A.

Since
A
is annihilated by an exterior power, the category
\mathcal{C}_{A}
is Tannakian; in other words, we have an equivalence of tensor categories
\mathcal{C}_{A}\cong\mathrm{Rep}(G)
for some
affine
algebraic group
G
(here
\mathrm{Rep}(G)

denotes
the category of finite-dimensional algebraic representations of
G
). We fix such an equivalence, so we can now think of
A
as a representation of
G
, with an underlying complex vector space. In fact,
A
generates
\mathrm{Rep}(G)
under taking tensor powers, direct sums and subquotients, which implies that
A
is a faithful representation of
G
.

Let
X:=Spec(A)
be the
affine

G
-scheme corresponding to
A
. Since
A
is a finite-dimensional
Frobenius algebra
over
{\mathbb{C}}
, we conclude that
X
is a finite set,
and
|X|=n
. The action of
G
on
X
induces a homomorphism
G\to Aut(X)=S_{X}
, which in turn induces a SM functor

\widetilde{F}_{A}:\mathrm{Rep}(S_{X})\to\mathcal{C}_{A},\;\;{\mathbb{C}}X\longmapsto A

as required.

∎

Theorem 8.3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
such that
\wedge^{N}\mathbf{V}=0
for some
N\geq 1
. Then there exists a finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-vector space
V
such that
\dim_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{V}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}V
and there exists a SM functor

\widetilde{F}_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathrm{Rep}(GL(V))\to\mathcal{C},\;\;\mathbf{V}_{n}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{V}}
,
G
be as in the proof of Lemma
8.3.1
, so that
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{V}}\cong\mathrm{Rep}(G)
; as before,
\mathbf{V}
can be considered as a faithful, finite-dimensional complex representation of the
affine
algebraic group
G
.

It is enough to establish that
\dim_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{V}=q^{n}
for some
n
, and the existence of a SM functor

\widetilde{F}_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(G)\cong\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{V}},\;\;\mathbf{V}_{n}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

Consider
\mathbf{V}
as a commutative and cocommutative Frobenius algebra in
\mathrm{Vect}_{{\mathbb{C}}}
; then
\mathbf{V}^{*}
is a a commutative and cocommutative Frobenius algebra as well, and a faithful representation of
G
.

Let
V:=Spec(\mathbf{V}^{*})
; this is a finite set with an action of
G
, and by the proof of Lemma
8.3.1
, the action of
G
on
V
induces an embedding
G\hookrightarrow S_{V}
.
The operators
\mu_{a}
(
a\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\times}
),
\dot{+}
and
z
on
\mathbf{V}
are morphisms of coalgebras by Relations
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
,
(

⁢
R
e

l
2

)
,
(

⁢
R
e

l
3

)
, hence they endow the
G
-set
V
with the structure of an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear vector space. In particular, this gives an embedding of finite groups
G\hookrightarrow GL(V)
, and we obtain

\dim_{\mathcal{C}}\mathbf{V}=\dim_{{\mathbb{C}}}\mathbf{V}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(V).

The embedding
G\hookrightarrow GL(V)
induces a SM functor

\widetilde{F}_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(G),\;\;\mathbf{V}_{n}\longmapsto\mathbf{V},

which completes the proof.
∎

The following corollary is proved in the same manner as Corollary
8.1.3
:

Corollary 8.3.3
.

The functor

Fun^{\otimes}\left(\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C},q^{n}),\;\;\widetilde{F}\longmapsto\widetilde{F}(\mathbf{V}_{n})

is an equivalence of categories. Here

•

Frob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C},q^{n})
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of
q^{n}
-dimensional
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius spaces
\mathbf{V}
annihilated by some exterior power, and isomorphisms between them.

•

Fun^{\otimes}\left(\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\mathcal{C}\right)
is the category of SM functors
\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathcal{C}
and natural SM isomorphisms.

Corollary 8.3.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
annihilated by some exterior power. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\dim\mathbf{V}=q^{n}
. Then the SM functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathcal{C}
seen in
8.1.1
factors through the functor
F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, and we have a natural SM isomorphism

F_{\mathbf{V}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{F}_{\mathbf{V}}\circ F_{n}.

Remark 8.3.5
.

In a subsequent article, we will show that for a pre-Tannakian symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
, and an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space
\mathbf{V}
in
\mathcal{C}

not
annihilated by any exterior power, the functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}
factors through a certain tensor category
\mathrm{Rep}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{ab}}}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
in which
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
sits as a full SM subcategory. This category
\mathrm{Rep}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{ab}}}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is called the
abelian envelope
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
and it is expected to be similar to the Deligne tensor categories
Rep^{ab}(\underline{S}_{t})
,
Rep^{ab}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{C}}))
seen in
[
CO2
,
D2
,
EHS
]
.

9.
Injective representations of infinite general linear group

We now study the analogue of Sam-Snowden’s category of algebraic representations of
S_{\infty}
(see
[
SS
]
) for the group
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
. We restrict ourselves here to the full subcategory in the category of
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
-modules whose objects are tensor powers of the natural representation
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}={\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}
. Our main result is that this category is the universal SM category generated by an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space.

9.1.
A subcategory of the Deligne category

Definition 9.1.1
.

For any
s,k\geq 0
, denote

Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}=\{R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\;\text{ linear subspace, where }\;R\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\;\text{ is surjective }\}.

This is a subset of
Rel_{s,k}
as defined in Section
4
. Recall the operations defined in Section
3
.

Lemma 9.1.2
.

Let
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
. Then

d(R,S)=0,\;\;S\star R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,l}.

Furthermore, for
R_{1}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{1},k_{1}}
,
R_{2}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{2},k_{2}}
, consider
R_{1}\times R_{2}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s_{1}+s_{2}+k_{1}+k_{2}}
. Then
R_{1}\times R_{2}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{1}+s_{2},k_{1}+k_{2}}
.

Proof.

Using the notation of Definition
3.2.1
we have for
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}

\dim\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ projection of }(R,0)+(0,S)\\
\text{ on the subspace }{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l+k+s}\end{subarray}\right)=k

which implies
d(R,S)=0
.

For
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
, there exist
A\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{d_{1}\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B\in Mat_{l\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B^{\prime}\in Mat_{d_{2}\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
such that

R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}\\
A^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\;\;S=Row\begin{bmatrix}-B&I_{l}\\
B^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix}\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\Longrightarrow}}\;\;S\circledast R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}&0\\
A^{\prime}&0&0\\
0&-B&I_{l}\\
0&B^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix}

Multiplying the above matrix by the invertible matrix
\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}&0&0&0\\
0&I_{d_{1}}&0&0\\
B&0&I_{l}&0\\
-B^{\prime}&0&0&I_{d_{2}}\end{bmatrix}
, we obtain:

S\circledast R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}&0\\
A^{\prime}&0&0\\
-BA&0&I_{l}\\
B^{\prime}A&0&0\end{bmatrix}\;\Longrightarrow\;S\star R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-BA&I_{l}\\
A^{\prime}&0\\
B^{\prime}A&0\end{bmatrix}

Thus the projection of
S\star R
to
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s}
in
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{l},0,{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s})
is surjective. The last statement of the lemma is obvious.
∎

Let us consider a (
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear) category
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
whose objects are
[k]
,
k\geq 0
, with morphism spaces
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{I}_{\infty}}([s],[k]):={\mathbb{C}}Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
, where we denote the morphism corresponding to
R\in Rel^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\infty}}}_{s,k}
by
f_{R}
. The composition of morphisms defined as follows: for
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
, we define

f_{S}\circ f_{R}:=f_{S\star R}.

We also define the monoidal structure on
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
as follows: we set
[l]\otimes[k]:=[l+k]
, and tensor products of morphisms given by
f_{R_{1}}\otimes f_{R_{2}}:=f_{R_{1}\times R_{2}}
, just like in Section
4
.

The symmetric structure on
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
is again given by the morphisms

s=f_{R^{\prime}}:[l]\otimes[k]\to[k]\otimes[l]
where

R^{\prime}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},-b_{1},\ldots,-b_{k},-a_{1},\ldots,-a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\in Rel^{\infty}_{k+l,k+l}.

Clearly, for each
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
, we obtain a SM embedding
\Gamma_{t}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\hookrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
which is not full. When
t=q^{n}
, we can compose
\Gamma_{t=q^{n}}
with the specialization functor

F_{n}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t=q^{n}}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\;\;\mathbf{V}_{t=q^{n}}\longmapsto\mathbf{V}_{n}

to obtain a SM functor

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{n}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})),\;\;[1]\longmapsto\mathbf{V}_{n}.

9.2.
Representations of the infinite general linear group

Definition 9.2.1
.

(1)

Let
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
, defined with respect to the embedding
GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})\subset GL_{n+1}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
given by
A\mapsto\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
.

(2)

Let
\iota_{n}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1}
be the natural embedding, given by adding a coordinate
0
at the end. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V:=}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}=\bigcup_{n\neq 0}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
; this is the countable-dimensional vector space
consisting of infinite sequences of elements in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
which have finite support
(i.e. sequences
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
,
a_{i}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
and
a_{i}=0
for
i>>1
). We will denote by
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}
the subspace of sequences
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
in
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}
for which
a_{i}=0
for
i>n
.

We denote by
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}
the representation
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}
of
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

We will consider a natural basis for
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s}
given by elements of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}^{\times s}
written as
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s})
,
v_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}
.

For any
s\geq 0
, consider the embedding
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n+1}^{\otimes s}
given by
\iota_{n}:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1}
. By abuse of notation, we will denote the image of this embedding again by
\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}
.

(3)

In this section, we will denote by
f_{R}^{(n)}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
the map corresponding to
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}
as defined in Section
3
, which we previously denoted simply by
f_{R}
.

(4)

We denote by
L_{n},H_{n}\subset P_{n}\subset GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
the following subgroups:

By
P_{n}
we denote the parabolic subgroup of all matrices
\begin{bmatrix}C&A\\
0&B\end{bmatrix}\in GL_{n+k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
,
k\geq 0
where
C\in GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B\in GL_{k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
and
A\in Mat_{n\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
.

By
H_{n}\subset P_{n}
we denote the subgroup of all matrices as above for which
C
is the identity matrix of size
n\times n
.

We will also denote by
L_{n}\subset P_{n}
the Levi subgroup of
P_{n}
: the subgroup of all matrices as above for which
A=0
.

Denote by
\Gamma_{n}:Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
the following functor: for
W\in Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
,
\Gamma_{n}(W)
is given by the
H_{n}
-invariants in
Res_{P_{n}}^{GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}W
.

(5)

We define
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
to be the full subcategory of
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})-mod
whose objects are tensor powers of
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}
.

(6)

For
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
, let
\overline{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s}\to\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k}
be the morphism defined by

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\mapsto\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}^{\times k}\\
(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{V}}}\end{subarray}}w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}.

Since the projection
R\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
is surjective, this sum is actually finite.

Remark 9.2.2
.

The tensor powers of
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}
are admissible
GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
-representations in the sense of Nagpal
[
N2
]
: that is, every vector in such a representation is stabilized by
P_{n}
for some
n>0
.

Every admissible representation (in the sense of Nagpal
[
N2
]
) is a subquotient of a finite direct sum of tensor powers of
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}
.

Let us begin by showing why the sets
Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
occur in the context of representation stabilization.

Lemma 9.2.3
.

Let
s,k\geq 0
and let
n\geq k+s
. Let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{n+1}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n+1}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n+1}^{\otimes k})
, such that
f(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s})\subset\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
.
Then
f\in span\{f_{R}^{(n+1)}\,|\,R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}\}
.

For the proof of this lemma, will use the following straightforward statement:

Lemma 9.2.4
.

Let
k\geq 0
and let
U
be a vector space over
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

(1)

Let
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
. Then

\max_{(u_{1}|\ldots|u_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{U}}\dim span\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}=\dim R^{\perp}=k-\dim R.

(2)

Let
\underline{u}:=(u_{1}|\ldots|u_{k})\in U^{\times k}
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{\underline{u}}:=\{R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}\,|\,(u_{1}|\ldots|u_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{U}\}
. Then there exists
R\in\mathcal{R}_{\underline{u}}
such that
S\subset R
for any
S\in\mathcal{R}_{\underline{u}}
, and furthermore,

\dim R=k-\dim span\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}\}.

Proof of
Lemma

9.2.3
.

By
Section

3.1
, we may write
f=\sum_{R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}}\alpha_{R}f_{R}^{(n+1)}
for some
\alpha_{R}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Consider the partially ordered set
X=\{R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\,|\,\alpha_{R}\neq 0\},
the order given by inclusion. We need to prove that
X\subset Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
; equivalently, that any minimal element of
X
lies in
Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
.

Let
R\in X
be such a minimal element. Let
\pi:{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{s+k}\to{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
be the obvious projection. We need to show that
\pi(R)={\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
. We will now choose
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s},w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1}
such that

(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s}|w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})\in R^{\perp}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1}}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\dim span\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{s},w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}=k+s-\dim R.

Since
n\geq k+s
, one may choose
v_{1},\ldots,v_{s}\in\iota_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n})\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1}
. Assume that
\pi(R)\neq{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k}
; then we may also choose
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
such that at least one of them does not belong to
\iota_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n})
. The set
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}_{(v_{1}|\ldots|v_{s},w_{1}|\ldots|w_{k})}
as defined in
Lemma

9.2.4
contains
R
; since
R
is a minimal element in
X
, we actually have
\mathcal{R}\cap X=\{R\}.

Consider the decomposition of

f(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s})
with respect to the basis
({\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n+1})^{\times k}
of
\mathbf{V}_{n+1}^{\otimes k}
. The summand
w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k}
appears with coefficient

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{R}}\alpha_{S}
which is, by the computation above,
\alpha_{R}
. But
f(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s})\in\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
, so this coefficient must be
0
, contradicting that
R\in X
. Thus
R\in Rel_{s,k}^{\infty}
and the statement is proved.

∎

The functor
\Gamma_{n}
is clearly left-exact. It also behaves nicely on the subcategory
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
:

Lemma 9.2.5
.

\Gamma_{n}(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})=\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
and
\Gamma_{n}(\overline{f}_{R})={f}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(n)}}}_{R}
for any
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
.

Proof.

If a finite sum

\sum_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}}\alpha_{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}}v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{k}

belongs to
(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})^{H_{n}}
then
v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty}
. This proves the first statement.

In particular,
\Gamma_{n}(\overline{f}_{R})
is just the restriction of
\overline{f}_{R}
to the subspace

\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}\cong({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{n})^{\otimes k}\subset({\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{\infty})^{\otimes k}=\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k}.

∎

Corollary 9.2.6
.

The functor
\Gamma_{n}:\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is SM.

In fact, one can show (see also
[
N2
,
SS
]
) that the functor
\Gamma_{n}:Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is SM.

Proposition 9.2.7
.

There exists a functor
F:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
which sends

[k]\longmapsto\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k},\;\;f_{R}\longmapsto\overline{f}_{R}.

Furthermore, this functor is SM and is an equivalence of SM categories.

Proof.

We need to check that the following statements hold:

(1)

For
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
,
S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
, we have
\overline{f}_{S}\circ\overline{f}_{R}=\overline{f}_{S\star R}
.

(2)

For
R_{1}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{1},k_{1}}
,
R_{2}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{2},k_{2}}
, we have
\overline{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\overline{f}_{R_{2}}=\overline{f}_{R_{1}\times R_{2}}
.

(3)

\{\overline{f}_{R}:R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}\}
form a basis for
\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})

The fact that
\Gamma_{n}(\overline{f}_{R})=f^{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{(n)}}}_{R}
for any
n
immediately implies the first two statements above; it also shows that
\{\overline{f}_{R}:R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}\}
are linearly independent in
\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})
, since they are linearly independent for
n>>s+k
.

It remains to show that
\{\overline{f}_{R}:R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}\}
form a spanning set in
\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})
.

Indeed, let
f\in\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k})
. Then for any
n\geq 0
,
f(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s})\subset\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
(since these are
H_{n}
-invariants). By Lemma
9.2.3
, for
n>s+k
, the map
f\rvert_{\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}}:\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\to}}\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k}
can be uniquely written as
\sum_{R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}}\alpha_{R}^{(n)}f_{R}^{(n)}
for some
\alpha_{R}^{(n)}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Since
f_{R}^{(n+1)}\rvert_{\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s}}=f_{R}^{(n)}
for any
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
, we conclude that for any
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
, the scalar
\alpha^{(n)}_{R}
does not depend on
n
when
n\geq s+k
. This implies that
f\in span\{\overline{f}_{R}:R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}\}
and the proof is complete.

∎

Thus we obtain faithful SM functors
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
for every
t\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and (non-faithful) SM functors
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\to\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
for every
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
. We will denote such a functor again by
\Gamma_{t}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{n}
respectively.

Remark 9.2.8
.

We will see in
[
EH
]
that the objects
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}^{\otimes k}\in\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
are injective objects in the category
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
, and the full subcategory of
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
which is the additive idempotent (Karoubi) completion of
\mathcal{T}(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
in
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
is the full subcategory of injective objects in
Rep(GL_{\infty}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
.

9.3.
Universal property

Definition 9.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a SM category. An
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space
in
\mathcal{C}
is an object
\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{C}
equipped with the following
structures:

(
SemiStr_{1}
)

\mathbf{V}
is equipped with the structure of a coalgebra object in

\mathcal{C}
given by morphisms

m^{*}:\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2},\;\;\varepsilon^{*}:\mathbf{V}\to{\mathbbm{1}}

and by a multiplication operation
m:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathbf{V}
such that the following conditions hold:

(
SemiFr_{1}
)

(\mathbf{V},m^{*},\varepsilon^{*})
is a
cocommutative counital coalgebra object.

(
SemiFr_{2}
)

The
multiplication
m
is associative and commutative.

(
SemiFr_{3}
)

m,m^{*}
satisfy the Frobenius Relations and the Speciality Relation as in
(

⁢
S
t

r
1

)(

⁢
F

r
2

)
.

(
SemiStr_{2}
)

\mathbf{V}
is a module over the field
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. In other words,
\mathbf{V}
is equipped
with
maps

\displaystyle\dot{+}:\mathbf{V}\otimes\mathbf{V}\to\mathbf{V},

\displaystyle\mu:({\mathbb{F}}_{q},\cdot)\to(End_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}),\circ),\;\;a\mapsto\mu_{a},

\displaystyle z:{\mathbbm{1}}\to\mathbf{V}

satisfying the Relations
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
1

)
,
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
2

)
,
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
3

)
,
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
4

)
.

Furthermore, we require that the above structures satisfy Relations
(

⁢
R
e

l
1

)
,
(

⁢
R
e

l
2

)
,
(

⁢
R
e

l
3

)
,
(

⁢
R
e

l
4

)
.

Clearly, any
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space is also an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space.

Lemma 9.3.2
.

The object
[1]
in
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
is an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space.

Proof.

The object
[1]
is a Frobenius space in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}}
. Hence all the required properties of Definition
9.3.1
hold, provided the maps
m,m^{*},\varepsilon^{*},\dot{+},\mu
and
z
are defined by relations of the form
Rel_{s,k}^{\infty}
(morphisms in
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}
). This follows from their explicit definition given in Proposition
3.4.1
.
∎

Theorem 9.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a SM category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
. Then there exists a SM functor

\Gamma_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{C},\;\;[1]\longmapsto\mathbf{V}.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to the proof of Theorem
8.1.1
.
We may assume
\mathcal{C}
to be a strict rigid symmetric monoidal category. Then define a functor
\Gamma_{V}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{C}
by sending
[k]
to
\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}
and for each linear subspace
R\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{k+l}
,
\Gamma_{V}
sends
f_{R}:[k]\to[l]
to
\hat{f}_{R}:\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\to\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}
, as defined in Definition
9
below.

We then extend
\Gamma_{V}
by linearity to the entire space
\operatorname{Hom}([k],[l])
.

In order to ensure that this is indeed a functor, we need to check that for any
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k},S\in Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
,
\Gamma_{V}(f_{S}\circ f_{R})=\hat{f}_{S}\circ\hat{f}_{R}
. This is proved in Proposition
9.4.4
.

Now, to check that this is a monoidal functor, we only need to check that
\Gamma_{V}(f_{R_{1}}\otimes f_{R_{2}})=\hat{f}_{R_{1}}\otimes\hat{f}_{R_{2}}
for any linear subspaces
R_{1}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{1},k_{1}},R_{2}\in Rel^{\infty}_{s_{2},k_{2}}
. This is proved in Lemma
9.4.5
. It remains to show that this functor is symmetric, which follows from the fact the the symmetry morphism

\sigma\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k},\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathbf{V}^{\otimes l})
is given by
\hat{f}_{R^{\prime}}
where

R^{\prime}=\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l},b_{1},\ldots,b_{k},-b_{1},\ldots,-b_{k},-a_{1},\ldots,-a_{l})|\,\forall i,j,\;a_{i},b_{j}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{q}\}\subset{\mathbb{F}}_{q}^{2k+2l}.

∎

Corollary 9.3.4
.

The functor

Fun^{\otimes}_{iso}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\infty},\mathcal{C}\right)\longrightarrow SFrob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C}),\;\;\Gamma\longmapsto\Gamma([1])

is an equivalence of categories. Here

•

SFrob_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}(\mathcal{C})
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius spaces in
\mathcal{C}
and isomorphisms between them.

•

Fun^{\otimes}_{iso}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\underline{\mathrm{Rep}}(GL_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))}},\mathcal{C}\right)
is the category of SM functors
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\to\mathcal{C}
and natural SM isomorphisms.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Corollary
8.1.3
.
∎

Remark 9.3.5
.

If the object
\mathbf{V}\in\mathcal{C}
is actually rigid, of dimension
t
(that is, it is an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear Frobenius space, and not just semi-Frobenius), then Theorem
8.1.1
implies that we have a symmetric monoidal functor
F_{\mathbf{V}}:\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
whose restriction to the subcategory
\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\subset\mathcal{T}(\underline{GL}_{t}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
gives a symmetric monoidal functor

\Gamma^{\prime}:\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

This functor is equal to
\Gamma_{V}
by Corollary
9.3.4
.
In particular, in that case the morphisms
\hat{f}_{R}
in
\mathcal{C}
described in Definition
9
will coincide with the morphisms
\widetilde{f}_{R}
defined in Section
7
.

9.4.
Diagrams for the semi-Frobenius spaces

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a SM category, and let
\mathbf{V}
be an
{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
-linear semi-Frobenius space in
\mathcal{C}
.

In this setting, we still have all the constructions of Section
5.1
except for the morphisms
\varepsilon
and
coev
(note that the morphism

z^{*}:=ev\circ(z\otimes\operatorname{Id})=ev\circ(\operatorname{Id}\otimes z)

is still defined!).

Consequently, all the statements of Sections
6.1
,
6.2
and
6.3
will hold.

In this setting, we will define morphisms
\hat{f}_{R}
in
\mathcal{C}
corresponding to
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
, which will be the analogues of the morphisms defined in Section
9.1
,
9.2
.

Example 9.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Rep}(GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}))
and let
\mathbf{V}:=\mathbf{V}_{n}
. Then for any
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
there exist matrices
A\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{d\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
such that

R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}\\
A^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\,\,rk(A^{\prime})=d.

The map
f_{R}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{GL_{n}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})}(\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}_{n}^{\otimes k})
can now be described as follows:

v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{s}\;\mapsto\;\delta_{A^{\prime}\vec{v},\,\dot{0}}\,w_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes w_{k},\;\;\;\text{ where }\;\;\;\vec{v}:=\begin{bmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\\
\vdots\\
v_{s}\end{bmatrix},\;\;\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\\
\vdots\\
w_{k}\end{bmatrix}:=A\vec{v},

Here we used notation as defined in Section
2.1
.

Definition 9.4.2
.

Let
R\in Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
. We define a morphism
\hat{f}_{R}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathbf{V}^{\otimes s},\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k})
as follows: let
A\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{d\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be such that

R=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}\\
A^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\,\,rk(A^{\prime})=d

Then we set

\hat{f}_{R}:=\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\right)\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A\\
A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}

Diagrammatically,
\hat{f}_{R}
is given by

(9)

Explicitly, this means that
\hat{f}_{R}
looks like this:

{A_{1}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{A_{2}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{A_{k}}
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{A^{\prime}_{1}}
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
{A^{\prime}_{d}}
\circ
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot
\cdot

We first check that
\hat{f}_{R}
is well defined:

Lemma 9.4.3
.

The morphism
\hat{f}_{R}
doesn’t depend on the choice of
A,A^{\prime}
as above.

Proof.

Let
B\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B^{\prime}\in Mat_{d\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be another pair of matrices such that

R=Row\begin{bmatrix}B&I_{k}\\
B^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\,rk(B^{\prime})=d.

Then we can find some
D\in GL_{d}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
and
D^{\prime}\in Mat_{k\times d}
for which

\begin{bmatrix}B\\
B^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}&D^{\prime}\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}A\\
A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus we only need to show that

\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\right)\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{k}&D^{\prime}\\
0&D\end{bmatrix}}=\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d}\right)

which is just
\mu_{[I_{k}\,D^{\prime}]}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes z^{\otimes d}\right)
.
By Corollary
6.2.11
, we have:

\mu_{[I_{k}\,D^{\prime}]}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes z^{\otimes d})=\mu_{[I_{k}\,I_{k}]}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\left(\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ z^{\otimes d}\right)\right)

Now, by Relations
(

⁢
S
t

r
2

)(

⁢
L
i

n
3

)
, we have:

\mu_{D^{\prime}}\circ z^{\otimes d}=z^{\otimes k}=\mu_{[\dot{0}]}\circ z^{\otimes k},

where
[\dot{0}]
denotes the
k\times k
zero matrix. So

\mu_{[I_{k}\,D^{\prime}]}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes z^{\otimes d})=\mu_{[I_{k}\,I_{k}]}\circ\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes\left(\mu_{[\dot{0}]}\circ z^{\otimes k}\right)\right)=\mu_{[I_{k}\,0]}\circ(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}}^{\otimes k}\otimes z^{\otimes d})

In terms of diagrams, we obtained that the left hand side of (
10
) equals

Proposition 9.4.4
.

Let
R_{1}\subset Rel^{\infty}_{s,k}
and
R_{2}\subset Rel^{\infty}_{k,l}
. Then

\hat{f}_{R_{2}}\circ\hat{f}_{R_{1}}=\hat{f}_{R_{2}\star R_{1}}

where
R_{2}\star R_{1}
are defined in Definition
3.2.1
.

Proof.

Let
d_{i}=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}R_{i}
,
r_{i}:=s_{i}+k_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

Let
A\in Mat_{k\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),A^{\prime}\in Mat_{d_{1}\times s}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
,
B\in Mat_{l\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q}),B^{\prime}\in Mat_{d_{2}\times k}({\mathbb{F}}_{q})
be such that

R_{1}=Row\begin{bmatrix}-A&I_{k}\\
A^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\;R_{2}=Row\begin{bmatrix}-B&I_{l}\\
B^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix},\;\;rk(A^{\prime})=d_{1},\,rk(B^{\prime})=d_{2}.

As we saw in the proof of Lemma
9.1.2
, we have:

R_{2}\star R_{1}=Row\begin{bmatrix}-BA&I_{l}\\
A^{\prime}&0\\
B^{\prime}A&0\end{bmatrix}=Row\begin{bmatrix}-BA&I_{l}\\
B^{\prime}A&0\\
A^{\prime}&0\end{bmatrix}.

Recall that by Proposition
6.1.2
, we have the following equalities (for equality of appropriate diagrams, see Equation (
9
)):

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{R_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d_{1}}\right)\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A\\
A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}\;=\;\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d_{1}}\right)\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}A&0\\
0&A^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{s}\\
I_{s}\end{bmatrix}}

\displaystyle=\;\left(\operatorname{Id}_{\mathbf{V}^{\otimes k}}\otimes(z^{*})^{\otimes d_{1}}\right)\,\circ\,\left(\mu_{A}\otimes\mu_{A}^{\prime}\right)\,\circ\,\mu_{\begin{bmatrix}I_{s}\\
I_{s}\end{bmatrix}}

The statement which we want to prove,
\hat{f}_{R_{2}}\circ\hat{f}_{R_{1}}=\hat{f}_{R_{2}\star R_{1}}
can
then
be interpreted as:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{113.27621pt}{-37.36133pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{64}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
